---
# Finding: The three fundamental meaning gaps of Communication Craft

## Changes from Prior

**Prior path:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_15-47__essence-formation-method/finding.md

**Revision trigger:** User correction. The earlier inquiry was framed around one case: the user's operational briefings to a manager. The user asked for a generic view of Communication Craft as a whole, and for its three most fundamental meaning gaps.

**What's preserved:**
- The essence-formation ideas are kept as inputs to the second gap below (the meaning):
  - answers reached by different kinds of "moves";
  - companions: the reasons and limits that travel with an answer;
  - coverage vs sufficiency;
  - backward formation from a draft.

**What's changed:**
- The question is no longer "how is an essence formed for one exchange".
- It is now "which undefined concepts does every Communication Craft operation rest on".
- The operational case is now evidence only.

**What's new:**
- Three gaps, a test for "fundamental", a hard core for each gap, closure criteria, and how every other candidate concept reduces to the three.

**Migration:** none. No skills or files depend on the prior finding's wording.

## Question

What are the three most fundamental meaning gaps in Communication Craft?

**Communication Craft** is the communication part of Workcraft, the project's work methodology: the theory and the skills for:
- creating communication;
- receiving it;
- assessing it;
- improving it;
- transforming it (shortening, replying, translating, re-targeting).

A **meaning gap** here is a concept the craft depends on but has not defined well enough to build on. The user wants three things:
- the three gaps;
- why each is fundamental;
- what it would take to close each, so they can be well defined before the framework is built.

The answer must hold for any kind of communication, not only operational reports.

## Finding Summary

The three fundamental meaning gaps are:

1. **The need: what a communication must achieve, and for whom.**
   - We have a working definition only for one family: "the recipient's next act, and the questions it raises".
   - It does not yet cover:
     - several parties with different needs;
     - unknown or later readers;
     - purposes that are not acts (thanks, condolence, keeping a relationship).
2. **The meaning: what the communication conveys, as something we can hold and check.**
   - Today "essence" means "the important meaning for the present use". That is circular with the need, and it is allowed to stay unwritten.
   - Meanwhile our main check needs it to be quotable.
   - It has no defined unit, and no required attachments (reasons, limits, status, basis).
3. **Conveyance: what a text actually makes available to its recipient.**
   - Only one corner is defined: the explicitness test ("does the text single out what its wording needs?").
   - The core is undefined: **what a text may legitimately leave unsaid**.

**Why these three are fundamental.**
- **Every Communication Craft check is a comparison.** A text is compared against its meaning; a meaning against the need; a meaning against its sources. The need and the meaning are the two abstract things every comparison relies on. Conveyance is the relation every readability and receiving question is about.
- **Every observed failure family traces to one of them being undefined:**
  - polished messages that missed what the recipient needed;
  - silent decisions and dropped reasons;
  - "of what?" gaps and spreading exemptions in the explicitness detector.

**Each gap has one hard core to define first:**
- the **unit of need** (a question to answer, or a state to reach);
- the **unit of meaning** (an answer, act or stance, with its attachments and its relations to other units);
- the **boundary of the implicit**.

**Everything else we considered reduces to these three:**
- **"Adequacy"** (is the communication good enough?) is not a fourth gap. It is the verdict over the three, plus one grounded check that the need was stated completely.
- **Grounds and authority** (who settled a meaning, on what basis) belong inside the meaning, as each unit's status and basis.
- **Preservation, importance, shared knowledge, reception, time and register** all reduce to the three.

**Order.** First define the need unit and the meaning unit together, because each must fit the other. Then the three gaps can be closed in parallel.

**Confidence limit.** That the three apply to any genre is argued from constructed cases (a condolence note, API documentation, an AI-agent prompt, a translation). It has not been tested on real held-out texts. That test is the first next action.

## Finding

### 1. Why we are asking this

The communication work so far produced:
- one measured capability: the explicitness question bank and detector;
- several concept findings:
  - the **communication substrate**: a 14-facet map of what can matter in an exchange;
  - the **essence**: the important meaning for the present use;
  - the **recipient's need**;
  - the split between checking the *text* and checking the *content*.

Each new skill so far has quietly invented its own standard of "good". The operational checks are the clearest example: each one mixed "is it well worded?" with "is it true and sensible?". An AI pointed at them produced fluent rewrites and missed the real problems.

The user's request is to stop and identify the few concepts everything rests on, so the framework is built on defined ground.

### 2. The test for "fundamental"

A gap counts as fundamental only if it passes both tests:
- **Dependency:** several Communication Craft operations or checks cannot be defined or run without it.
- **Failure:** observed failures trace back to it being undefined.

Twelve candidates were gathered:
- from the earlier findings;
- from the five operations;
- from observed failures;
- from neighbouring fields: rhetoric, pragmatics, translation theory, requirements engineering, contract interpretation.

### 3. The key observation: every check is a comparison

Listing what each existing check compares exposes four things:

| Check | Compares | Against |
|---|---|---|
| Is the meaning well expressed? (materialization check) | the text | the meaning the author has settled |
| Does it answer what was needed? Is it complete, and without excess? (the fit checks) | the meaning | the recipient's need |
| Is it supported, and faithful to the original? (the fidelity checks) | the meaning | the sources, or the earlier version |
| Is it explicit and unambiguous? (the intelligibility checks) | the text | what the text itself makes available |

Two of the four things are concrete files: the **text** and the **sources**. The other two, the **need** and the **meaning**, are abstract, and both are thinly defined.

The **relation** between a text and what it conveys is the third thing everything leans on. Only one corner of it is defined.

These are the three gaps.

### 4. Gap 1: the need

**What it is.** What this communication must achieve, and for whom.

**What we have.** An earlier finding defines it, in its words, as *"what the recipient must be able to do after the exchange (their next act), and therefore which questions they must have answered."* The finding is devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_12-53__what-determines-the-view-combination-an-exchange-needs/finding.md, and that definition lives in its summary. It also says the need is never guessed: it is taken from the request, a standing arrangement, the workflow, asking the recipient, or a stated assumption, in that order.

**What is undefined.**
- **Whose need counts.**
  - Candidates: the recipient, the sender's purpose, the task, third parties, later readers.
  - When they conflict, how the conflict is shown rather than hidden.
- **What form a need takes.**
  - "Questions to be answered" works for briefings and decisions.
  - A condolence note or a thank-you has no questions. Its need is a state to reach ("the recipient feels their loss is recognised").
- **How a need is found when there is no request and no known reader.** Examples: reference documents, standing documents, broadcasts.
- **How a need is stated so that coverage can be tested.** It must be stated as questions or states, not topics. A message can cover every topic and still leave the recipient unable to act.

**Why it is fundamental.** The need decides:
- what to select;
- what counts as important;
- what the fit checks measure;
- whether the result is usable;
- what a transformation must preserve.

The best-known failures are needs that were missed or guessed: polished briefings the reader couldn't use, and the AI silently choosing the level of detail.

**Hard core:** the unit of need.

**Closed when:**
- one stated form expresses the needs of at least five genres without forcing them;
- the form exposes the "covered every topic, still unusable" failure;
- there is a rule for whose need governs and how conflicts are recorded.

### 5. Gap 2: the meaning

**What it is.** What the communication conveys, held as an object that can be quoted, checked and preserved.

**What we have.** The substrate finding (devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_22-09__communication-substrate-foundation-and-dimensions/finding.md) says: *"Essence names the important meaning and relationships in that account for the present use."* It also says the essence *"need not designate"* a separate document.

The materialization-vs-content finding (devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_13-08__materialization-check-vs-content-check-in-communication-craft/finding.md) decides what kind of problem a gap is by whether the right meaning can be *quoted* from what the author has settled.

These two do not fit together:
- an essence that is allowed to stay unwritten cannot be quoted;
- "important for the present use" depends on the need, which is itself undefined generically.

**What is undefined.**
- **The unit.**
  - What is one piece of meaning: a claim, an answer to a need question, an act (a request, a refusal), a stance, an open item?
  - **The relations between pieces.** Examples: "therefore", "unless", "in contrast". The substrate finding notes that much of the meaning lives in these.
- **What must travel with each unit:**
  - its **companions**: reasons, conditions, qualifications, limits;
  - its **status**: settled by the author; gathered from a source; produced fresh; left open on purpose; to be asked; proposed by an AI but not confirmed; found in a draft; attributed by a reader; superseded;
  - its **basis**: which source, which person.
- **Held meaning vs this communication's meaning.**
  - Held meaning is everything known or settled.
  - This communication's meaning is what is selected or produced for this need.
- **When the exact wording is itself the meaning.** Examples: quotations, "might" vs "will", contractual terms.

**Why it is fundamental.**
- It is the object of:
  - the content check;
  - the target of the wording check;
  - what fidelity and preservation protect;
  - what formation produces;
  - what receiving reconstructs.
- The failures traced to it are:
  - decisions made silently, with no status recorded;
  - dropped second reasons and lost limits (missing companions);
  - checks that mixed kinds, because there was no meaning object to check against.

**Hard core:** the unit of meaning, with its attachments and relations.

**Closed when:**
- the units represent three different genres without losing their relations;
- a dropped reason or an unconfirmed AI decision shows up as a missing attachment or a status;
- the definition agrees with the quote test from the materialization-vs-content finding.

### 6. Gap 3: conveyance

**What it is.** What a text actually makes available to its recipient: stated, connected, and singled out. Also what it may leave to things outside the text.

"Conveyance" is a working label. The plain description is the definition.

**What we have.** The explicitness bank (the project's question bank for finding places where the wording needs something the text doesn't single out) defines one corner: two tests, demand and sufficiency, judged on the text alone. It is the only closed piece of Communication Craft. It closed by defining a relation precisely, not by collecting more examples.

**What is undefined.**
- **What counts as supplied.**
  - Candidates: the text, the stated setting, earlier turns in the exchange, conventions of the genre, declared shared ground.
  - What never counts: undeclared reader knowledge. This keeps the user's earlier correction ("detect the text's explicitness, not reader knowledge").
- **The principled boundary of what may be left implicit.**
  - The bank's record of persistent misses shows exemption rules spreading: "there is one end state", "the point is about the whole".
  - Each was decided case by case, because this boundary has no principle.
- **What conveyance covers beyond singling things out:**
  - connections and order;
  - which act the text performs (is it a request or a report?);
  - meaning the text conveys that was not intended.
- **How receiving rebuilds meaning from a text,** and how to check the rebuild.

**Why it is fundamental.** It underlies:
- every intelligibility check;
- every review of a written text;
- the entire receiving direction.

The failures traced to it are the explicitness detector's gaps and its spreading exemptions.

**Hard core:** the boundary of the implicit.

**Closed when:**
- one stated principle decides the detector's exemption cases that are now handled by separate rules;
- each intelligibility peer maps to a conveyance question. The peers are ambiguity, vagueness, connection, order and implication, from the 15-peer problem taxonomy.

### 7. How everything else reduces to the three

| Candidate | Reduces to |
|---|---|
| **Adequacy** ("is it good enough?") | The verdict over the three: every need unit is answered or marked open, every answer is grounded, and every answer is conveyed. Plus one **grounded sufficiency probe**: a fresh reader with only the text answers the need's questions, and the answers are compared quote by quote with the meaning. The probe catches a need that was stated incompletely. |
| **Grounds and authority** | Each meaning unit's status and basis. |
| **Preservation** in transformations | The same held meaning, under a new need, with a list of units that must survive. Translation theory treats the purpose of the new text as what decides this, which supports the reduction. |
| **Importance / selection** | The need selecting meaning units. |
| **Shared knowledge** | Conveyance's "what counts as supplied". |
| **Reception** | Conveyance run backwards. |
| **Time** (meaning changing over turns) | Successive needs and texts, with meaning units marked "superseded". |
| **Register / relationship** | A state-type need ("feels respected") + a stance unit in the meaning + tone as wording-bound conveyance. This works only if state-type needs work. |
| **The unit of communication; the boundary with other Workcraft concepts; openness** | The meaning unit; the existing text-vs-content split; the "left open" status. |

Requirements engineering ended up with the same three foundations:
- validation (is it the right thing?) is the need against the meaning;
- the specification is the meaning;
- verification (is it built right?) is the meaning against the text.

Its main closing tool was **traceability**: every requirement traces to a need, every part of the product to a requirement. That transfers directly here:
- every meaning unit traces to a need unit;
- every meaning unit traces to the place in the text that conveys it.

### 8. A possible shape for how the gaps close (hypothesis only)

Combining the three suggests one structure:
- a list of **need units**;
- **meaning units** that answer them, each with its companions, status and basis, and with the relations between them;
- for each meaning unit, **the place in the text that conveys it**, or a mark that it isn't conveyed.

Every existing check becomes one comparison along one of those links. Receiving is the same structure filled in backwards from the text.

This is offered as **a hypothesis to test, not a definition.** The substrate finding deliberately deferred a universal schema. The structure has not yet been built for a real text.

### 9. Order of work

- **Define first, jointly:** the need unit and the meaning unit. They form a contract: whatever a need unit is, meaning units must be able to answer it.
- **Then in parallel:**
  - the rest of the need (whose need; sourcing without a request);
  - the meaning's attachments and status vocabulary;
  - conveyance's boundary of the implicit.

  The boundary of the implicit does not depend on the meaning unit, because it is judged on the text.
- **In a single case,** start from the need. **Across the framework,** the meaning is the most depended-on gap.

## Inherited Commitments Re-test

- **Commitment:** the communication substrate is a working understanding, and the essence is "the important meaning for the present use"; it need not be a separate document.
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_22-09__communication-substrate-foundation-and-dimensions/finding.md, sections 1 and 2.
  - **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:**
    - The working-understanding view holds.
    - But "important for the present use" depends on an undefined need.
    - Allowing an unwritten essence conflicts with the quote test in the materialization-vs-content finding.
    - So the essence becomes Gap 2 (the meaning): it must be holdable and quotable whenever a check depends on it.
- **Commitment:** the 14 facets serve as a guide to attention, not a template.
  - **Source:** same substrate finding, sections 2 and 3.
  - **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** none of the three gaps replaces the facets. Facets remain the sweep for what might matter. Each facet maps to a need, meaning or conveyance concern; for example, "reception" maps to conveyance.
- **Commitment:** the loop "establish essence → materialize → review → diagnose → revise", and "fix what is actually at fault".
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_19-33__communication-substrate-materialization-and-review-loop/finding.md, summary.
  - **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** each loop step is a comparison along one of the three gaps; diagnosis routes to the gap at fault.
- **Commitment:** the recipient's need is the next act → questions → views, and the need is sourced, never guessed.
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_12-53__what-determines-the-view-combination-an-exchange-needs/finding.md, summary.
  - **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:**
    - The sourcing order holds.
    - "Next act → questions" holds for act-driven exchanges only.
    - Condolence notes, reference documents and multi-party exchanges fall outside it, which is why it is Gap 1 (the need).
    - The finding's own required test on unseen exchanges was never run.
- **Commitment:** the materialization check vs the content check, decided by where the right meaning can be quoted from.
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_13-08__materialization-check-vs-content-check-in-communication-craft/finding.md, summary.
  - **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** it is one of the comparisons in section 3 of this finding, and it presupposes Gap 2. This finding names that presupposition. It does not change the check.
- **Commitment:** essence formation by moves; companions; coverage vs sufficiency; backward formation; a preservation map.
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_15-47__essence-formation-method/finding.md (the prior finding).
  - **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:**
    - The moves become status values of meaning units.
    - Companions become required attachments.
    - Sufficiency becomes the grounded probe under adequacy.
    - The preservation map becomes the preservation reduction.
    - The operational framing is dropped.
- **Commitment:** meaning and fidelity should be part of development from the beginning; develop through concrete uses.
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_12-40__communication-substrate-development-order/finding.md (written by another AI).
  - **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:**
    - Gap 2 (the meaning) is exactly what makes meaning and fidelity definable early.
    - The closure criteria require tests on concrete texts, which matches "develop through use".
- **Commitment:** the three problem families (intelligibility, fidelity, fit) and their 15 peers.
  - **Source:** /Users/nsstorm/Desktop/projects/AIOpsMan/compare/devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-05_18-49__peer-categories-of-explicitness-in-communication-problems/finding.md.
  - **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:**
    - The families map to comparisons:
      - intelligibility is the text against conveyance;
      - fidelity is the meaning against its sources;
      - fit is the meaning against the need.
    - The families are therefore views over the three gaps, not independent foundations.

## Next Actions

### MUST

- **What:** Test the three gaps on at least four real texts from genres not used here: a relational message, reference documentation, a prompt to an AI agent, and a summary or translation. For each, write down the need, the meaning and the conveyance problems, and look for anything that fits none of them.
  - **Who:** the next Communication Craft inquiry, with the user choosing the texts.
  - **Gate:** before any of the three gaps is defined in a reference file.
  - **Why:** the claim that the three hold for any genre currently rests on constructed cases only.
- **What:** Define the need unit and the meaning unit together, including state-type needs and relations between meaning units.
  - **Who:** a dedicated inquiry.
  - **Gate:** after the genre test above.
  - **Why:** both gaps depend on this contract. Register's reduction also depends on state-type needs working.

### COULD

- **What:** State one principle for what a text may leave implicit, and re-decide the explicitness bank's exemption rules by it.
  - **Who:** the next bank version.
  - **Gate:** the next time the bank is revised.
  - **Why:** it closes the core of Gap 3 (conveyance) and targets the bank's recorded persistent misses.
  - **Depends-on:** MUST item "genre test". OVERRIDE: adoption-ready despite the open MUST. Reason: the boundary of the implicit is judged on the text alone, and the bank's misses are already documented, so this work does not wait on the need/meaning definitions.
- **What:** Build the need / meaning / text-place structure (section 8) for three real texts, including one argument with "therefore / unless".
  - **Who:** a test inquiry.
  - **Gate:** after the need-unit / meaning-unit definition.
  - **Why:** decides whether the structure is the right closing form.
  - **Depends-on:** MUST item "define the need unit and meaning unit together". This COULD is GATED.
- **What:** Run the grounded sufficiency probe on the known "covered every topic, still unusable" case and two others.
  - **Who:** a test inquiry.
  - **Gate:** when the need-unit definition exists.
  - **Why:** confirms that adequacy needs no fourth gap.
  - **Depends-on:** MUST item "define the need unit and meaning unit together". This COULD is GATED.

### DEFERRED

- **What:** A universal schema or skill built on the three gaps.
  - **Gate:** after all three have passed their closure criteria.
  - **Why (if revived):** gives every skill one shared standard.

## Reasoning

**Why need, meaning and conveyance survived.**
- They are the only candidates that pass both tests (dependency and failure) and cannot be reduced to each other:
  - The need cannot be derived from the meaning: a correct, well-expressed meaning can answer the wrong need (the September report case).
  - Conveyance cannot be derived from the meaning: a perfect meaning can be badly conveyed.
- The strongest objection was that this is just the textbook "audience, message, style". It fails, for two reasons:
  - each gap is stated by what is undefined in *our* current concepts, with the definitions quoted;
  - each has a specific hard core and a closure test.
- A second objection was that the need is already defined. It fails because the existing definition's own words ("their next act") exclude whole genres, and its required test was never run.

**Killed: need, meaning, adequacy as the three.**
- Adequacy is a verdict, not a thing to compare. It needs the other two to mean anything.
- Its real content, "covered every topic but still unusable", is caught by stating the need as questions and adding one grounded sufficiency probe.

**Killed: authority as the one fundamental gap.**
- Clear authority does not tell the author what the recipient needs.
- The "of what?" gaps appeared in text the author had fully settled.
- Authority survives inside Gap 2 (the meaning), as who settled each unit. This matters more as AIs draft more, since "proposed by an AI, not confirmed" becomes the key status.

**Killed: time as a fourth gap.** Change over an exchange is successive needs, meanings and texts, with superseded units.

**Refined: the question-and-answer list.**
- A flat list of answers loses the relations ("therefore", "unless") that carry much of the meaning.
- It was refined to units plus relations, and demoted from definition to hypothesis.

**Kept with a condition: register.** It reduces to the three only if needs can be states, not just questions. If that fails, register returns as a candidate gap.

## Open Questions

### Monitoring

After the genre test, record any failure that does not fit need, meaning or conveyance. Two such failures of the same kind re-open the set.

### Research Frontiers

- Does the boundary of the implicit vary with the stakes of the need, so that more must be explicit when more is at risk?
- How can a receiver's rebuilt meaning be checked without vouching (asking a model to vouch for itself)?
- Will meaning status and authority become the dominant failure as AI drafting grows?
  - Promote this to a priority claim if it is the main failure in 3 of the next 5 real AI-drafted exchanges reviewed.

### Refinement Triggers

- **Re-open the set of three if:**
  - a real text has a failure that none of the three explains;
  - **or** state-type needs prove unworkable in the genre test, which would make register a candidate fourth gap.
- **Re-open "adequacy is not a gap" if** a case passes all three comparisons and the sufficiency probe, yet the recipient still cannot use it.
- **Re-open the order of work if** the boundary of the implicit turns out to depend on the meaning unit.

# Finding: The need — what a communication must achieve, and for whom

## Changes from Prior

**Prior path:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_12-53__what-determines-the-view-combination-an-exchange-needs/finding.md

**Revision trigger:** scope change. The three-gaps finding (devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_15-59__three-fundamental-meaning-gaps-of-communication-craft/finding.md) named "the need" as the first fundamental gap of Communication Craft. The prior definition only worked for briefings and decisions. The user asked to go deep on it without over-defining it.

**What's preserved:**
- The need is never guessed.
- The sourcing order.
- Questions as the working unit for informational messages.
- Views as labels on questions.
- The act table as defaults.
- The need is distinct from the sender's act and from the relationship.

**What's changed:**
- "Next act" is widened to "the state the communication should bring about".
- One recipient becomes several parties in three roles.
- Requirements now include conditions and constraints, not only questions.
- A quotable genre or document-role convention is added as a source.
- On register (voice and tone), the prior finding said "register depends on the relationship, not the need". It is now partly need-driven: relational messages have conditions.

**What's new:**
- the state / requirement split;
- the holder / setter / affected roles;
- the "request is evidence" rule;
- the grounded implied-state diagnostic.

**Migration:** none. No skill implements the prior definition yet.

**MUST/COULD drift:**
- **Prior MUST:** "Write one short recipient-need base reference, with a view table inside it, and test it on held-out real exchanges."
- **New MUST:** test the widened model on held-out real texts from six genres before writing any reference.
- **Rationale:** the reference's content changed (states, conditions, parties), so testing must come first.

## Question

**What is a communication's "need": what it must achieve, and for whom?** And what is one unit of need: a question to answer, or a state the recipient should reach?

The prior definition was "the recipient's next act → the questions it raises". It does not cover three cases:
- several parties wanting different things;
- unknown or later readers, such as documentation;
- purposes that aren't acts, such as thanks, condolence, or keeping a relationship.

The answer had to:
- be generic across all communication;
- go deep;
- not over-define: understanding and tests, not a schema.

## Finding Summary

- **The question "question or state?" has a two-part answer: both, at different levels.**
  - **The state** is the destination: what should be true for a party after the communication. It is what you *derive from*.
  - **The requirements** are what the text must carry to get there. They are what you *check*.
- **A need is the state a communication should bring about, for each party who must reach it.**
  - It is set by someone with the standing to set it.
  - It is measured against what is already established between the parties: earlier messages, shared documents, the stated setting. It is never measured against what a reader privately knows.
- **The prior definition was already halfway there.** It reads "what the recipient must be able to do after the exchange, *and what they must understand* to do it". Its act list already included "stay informed" and "think together", which are states, not acts.
- **Requirements come in three kinds:**
  - **questions to answer:** informational messages (briefings, docs, instructions);
  - **conditions to meet:** expressive and relational messages. A thank-you must name what was done. A condolence must acknowledge the loss.
  - **constraints to respect:** must-nots. Don't commit the sender, don't reveal X, don't ask the bereaved for anything.
- **"For whom" splits into three roles:**
  - **holders:** who must reach a state (primary, secondary, later readers);
  - **the setter:** who has standing to decide what the message is for;
  - **the affected:** who impose constraints without reading it.
- **How the three uncovered cases fit:**
  - **Several parties:** several holders, each with a state. Conflicts are handled by putting the primary holder's requirements in the main message and others' in the supporting material, by splitting the message, or by recording the conflict. Constraints are never pushed down a layer.
  - **Unknown or later readers:** the need comes from what the document is *for*, taken from a quotable convention for its role (a decision record answers "why, which alternatives"). Otherwise it is stated visibly in the document ("this page is for developers integrating X").
  - **Thanks, condolence, relationships:** states whose requirements are mostly conditions. The core conditions come from the act's own point. Local ones (culture, relationship) must be sourced.
- **The request is evidence of the state, not the state itself.**
  - The September "operational view" request was a compressed version of the real state: "can decide whether to back the next steps".
  - Depart from the literal request only with a quoted source, or as a visible assumption the requester can correct.
- **The sender's purpose and the need fit together.**
  - The purpose should be stated as the *state*, never as a sender act ("report", "update").
  - The September failure was a purpose stated as an act.
- **Checking always goes through the text.** Quote where the text carries each requirement. A reader's inner state is unobservable at writing time in every genre, so expressive needs are no harder to check than informational ones.
- **A new diagnostic to test: the implied state versus the sourced state.**
  - From a draft, quote what it carries and list which requirements those quotes answer.
  - Then state what state that would actually achieve, and compare it with the sourced state.
- **Confidence limit.** The model was applied to constructed cases only (a PR description, an API reference, a condolence, and others). Testing it on real held-out texts is the first next step.

## Finding

### 1. Why this matters

Every Communication Craft check that asks "does this message fit?" needs something to compare against. That includes being responsive, being complete, and not carrying what nobody needs.

That something is the need. Our only definition came from briefings: the manager's "next act" and the questions it raises. It breaks on documentation, on thank-yous, and on messages with several readers.

So this inquiry asked what a need is in general, and what its smallest unit is.

### 2. The state: what a communication is for

**The common element in every case** is a state that should be true for some party after the communication, and that was not true before.

| Case | The state |
|---|---|
| Briefing | The manager can decide whether to back the next steps. |
| Documentation | A future developer can call the endpoint correctly. |
| Thanks | The helper knows that what they did was noticed and valued. |
| Condolence | The bereaved knows the loss is recognised, and that support is there without demand. |
| Warning | The on-call engineer knows the risk before their next shift, *so that* the outage is avoided. |
| Agent prompt | The agent completes the task within the constraints, and knows when it is done. |

**Three properties make a state statement useful.**

- **It names a party.** "Inform the team" names no state. "The on-call engineer knows the rollback changed" does.
- **It is set by someone with standing.** That might be the requester, a standing arrangement, the workflow, the document's role, or the sender for unsolicited messages. The setter is recorded, so a check can see when the sender set the state on behalf of someone else.
- **It is relative to what is established.** What is already settled between the parties does not need saying. Established means:
  - earlier turns in the conversation;
  - shared documents;
  - the stated setting.

  It never means what the reader privately knows. This keeps the earlier rule against guessing reader knowledge.

**A one-sentence precision test**, not a form: can you say who, after the communication, can, knows or feels what, and where the source for that is?

**Where the situation fits.** A need could be said to belong to the situation: "the outage must be avoided". But it can only be checked through a party who can act. So the state stays with a party, and the situation goes in a "so that" clause.

### 3. Requirements: what the text must carry

A state produces requirements. These are what checks compare against, because a text can be quoted for them.

**Questions to answer (informational states).**
- To *decide*, you need: what is to be decided, the recommendation and why, the options, the evidence and unknowns, and who does what next.
- The prior finding's act table stays as the default source of these questions. Views stay as labels on them.

**Conditions to meet (expressive and relational states).** A condition belongs to the need only if, without it, the state cannot be reached.

- **Core conditions** come from what the act itself is for:
  - **Thanking** is about something the other person did, so it must name it.
  - **Apologising** is about something the sender did, so it must own it.
  - **Condoling** is about the other person's loss, so it must acknowledge it and expect nothing back.

  These are close to universal.
- **Local conditions** must be sourced: how warm, how formal, what is customary in this team or culture. They come from a quotable convention or the relationship. Otherwise they are a visible assumption.
- This also settles the earlier disagreement about register (voice and tone). It is partly the need (relational conditions) and partly the relationship (local conventions).

**Constraints to respect (must-nots).**
- Examples:
  - do not commit the sender to something they can't promise;
  - do not reveal X;
  - do not ask anything of the bereaved;
  - stay within a length.
- They come from the setter or from affected parties.
- Conditions and constraints share one form ("must" / "must not"). They are kept apart because they come from different places.

**For an AI recipient**, the requirements are the acceptance criteria. The agent cannot reliably ask back "how will I know it's done?", so completeness matters more.

### 4. For whom: three roles

The prior definition had one recipient. Real communication has three roles.

| Role | What it is | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Holder** | a party who must reach a state (primary or secondary, present or later) | PR reviewer (primary), future maintainer (secondary) |
| **Setter** | who has standing to decide what the communication is for | the requester; a standing arrangement; the document's role; the sender for a warning |
| **Affected** | a party who constrains it without being the reader | the organisation (no secrets), a person mentioned in it |

**When holders conflict**, there are three options:
- **Layer:** the primary holder's questions and conditions go in the main message; secondary ones go in Workcraft's Supporting Context (its linked explanations and evidence).
- **Split** the communication.
- **Record** the conflict openly.

**Constraints are never layered.** A legal reviewer may read last, but their constraint holds everywhere.

### 5. How the need is found without guessing

The prior sourcing order holds. Two refinements are added.

**The order:**
1. the request;
2. a standing arrangement;
3. the workflow;
4. **a quotable genre or document-role convention** (new);
5. asking;
6. a visible assumption.

**A convention counts only if it can be quoted:**
- a style guide;
- a document-type template (for example, a decision record's "context, decision, consequences");
- the act's own point.

"Readers usually want…" is not a source. It becomes a visible assumption, written in the document.

**The request is evidence of the state, not the state itself.** People ask for a compressed version of what they need. Librarians handle this by asking "what will you use it for?".

In September, the request said "operational view". The standing arrangement held the real state: problems arrive "with a proposed outcome" so they can be "prioritized together". The finding behind this is devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_12-53__what-determines-the-view-combination-an-exchange-needs/finding.md.

Depart from the literal request only with a quoted source, or as a visible assumption the requester can correct.

**When an AI drafts with no request**, the visible assumption is the minimum discipline. Write down the assumed state where the author can see and correct it.

### 6. How the need is checked

**Quote where the text carries each requirement:**
- for questions: where each is answered;
- for conditions: where each is met;
- for constraints: that nothing violates them.

The reader's resulting state is never judged. It cannot be observed when writing, in any genre.

**The sufficiency probe from the three-gaps finding** generalises from "a fresh reader answers the need's questions" to "a fresh reader quotes where each requirement is carried".

**Diagnostic to test: implied versus sourced state.**
1. From a draft, quote what it carries.
2. List which requirements those quotes answer.
3. State the strongest state that this supports.
4. Compare it with the sourced state.

On the September message:
- the implied state was "the manager knows which documents exist";
- the sourced state was "the manager can decide whether to back the next steps".

The mismatch shows in one line. The quoting steps are what stop this from becoming a fluent but wrong model judgment.

### 7. Where the model stops

- **Aesthetic or experiential communication** (art, humour): the state is an experience, and its conditions are hard to state. This is outside work communication.
- **Exploratory exchanges** ("think it through together"): the state is discovered during the exchange. The model applies turn by turn, and the overall state stays open on purpose.

## Inherited Commitments Re-test

### Commitment 1: the need is the next act plus the questions it raises

- **Commitment:** "what the recipient must be able to do after the exchange, and what they must understand to do it"
- **Source:** 12-53, section 2.
- **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed, but its frame is revised.
- **Evidence:**
  - The definition's own "and what they must understand" clause is already a state.
  - Its act list includes "stay informed" and "think together".
  - "Act" is widened to "state", and questions become one of three requirement kinds.
  - Applying it literally to a condolence shows the gap: no act, and no questions the bereaved would ask.

### Commitment 2: the need is never guessed, and is sourced in order

- **Source:** 12-53, section 4.
- **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed, but its frame is revised.
- **Evidence:**
  - The order holds.
  - A quotable convention is inserted for unknown readers.
  - The request is reframed as evidence, using the September case's request vs standing arrangement.

### Commitment 3: the need is distinct from the sender's purpose and from the relationship

- **Source:** 12-53, section 3.
- **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed, but its frame is revised.
- **Evidence:**
  - Holds when purpose means a sender act.
  - When purpose is stated as the target state, purpose and need meet.
  - The September failure is a purpose stated as an act.

### Commitment 4: register depends on the relationship, not the need

- **Source:** 12-53, section 5.
- **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment found partly invalid.
- **Evidence:**
  - Relational messages have conditions on tone that come from the act (a condolence must not demand).
  - So register is partly need-driven, partly relationship-driven.
  - This also resolves the three-gaps finding's condition: "register reduces only if state-type needs work". They do.

### Commitment 5: the need is Gap 1, its hard core is the unit of need, and its closure criteria

- **Closure criteria (as stated in the three-gaps finding):**
  - 5 genres without forcing;
  - exposes "covered every topic, unusable";
  - whose need, and conflicts.
- **Source:** the three-gaps finding, section 4.
- **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed.
- **Evidence:**
  - The unit question is answered: two levels.
  - Applied to 6 constructed genres without forcing.
  - "Covered every topic" fails because topics are not requirements.
  - Roles and conflict handling are given.
  - The closure test on *real* texts is not yet met. It is the MUST below.

### Commitment 6: purposes, interests and needs; keep sources visible; represent conflicts

- **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_22-09__communication-substrate-foundation-and-dimensions/finding.md, facet 5 and section 7.
- **Status:** RE-TESTED. Commitment confirmed.
- **Evidence:**
  - Sources are recorded per state.
  - Conflicts are recorded.
  - "Interests" map to the affected role and its constraints.

## Next Actions

### MUST

- **What:** Apply the model to real held-out texts, one from each of six genres:
  - a briefing;
  - a PR description;
  - an API reference;
  - a thank-you or condolence;
  - a prompt to an AI agent;
  - an unsolicited warning.

  For each, write:
  - the holders;
  - the states with their sources;
  - the requirements;

  then quote where the text carries each requirement.
  - **Who:** the next need inquiry; the user picks the texts.
  - **Gate:** before writing any need reference file.
  - **Why:** the model has only met constructed cases.

### COULD

- **What:** Test the implied-state vs sourced-state diagnostic on three real drafts, including one AI draft.
  - **Who:** a test inquiry.
  - **Gate:** alongside the genre test.
  - **Why:** it could become the cheapest check for "well written but misses the point".
  - **Depends-on:** MUST "held-out genre test". OVERRIDE: adoption-ready despite the open MUST. Reason: the diagnostic uses only quoting and the sourced state, so it can be tested on drafts independently.
- **What:** Derive core conditions for thanks, apology, condolence, congratulation and check-in from each act's point. Mark which conditions need local sources.
  - **Who:** a follow-up inquiry.
  - **Gate:** after the genre test.
  - **Why:** this is the part of the need with no prior coverage.
  - **Depends-on:** MUST "held-out genre test". This COULD is GATED.
- **What:** State how meaning units relate to requirements: they answer questions, meet conditions, respect constraints.
  - **Who:** the meaning-gap inquiry (Gap 2).
  - **Gate:** when Gap 2 starts.
  - **Why:** it is the need–meaning contract the three-gaps finding said must come first.

### DEFERRED

- **What:** Decide whether stakes (the cost of missing the state) set how explicit a text must be.
  - **Gate:** when the conveyance gap's boundary-of-the-implicit work starts.
  - **Why (if revived):** it may explain why some omissions are fine and others are not.
- **What:** Treat the state sentence as the brief a human gives an AI drafter.
  - **Gate:** when an AI-drafting skill is designed.

## Reasoning

**Why "both, at two levels" beat "question" or "state".**
- Questions alone cannot express a condolence. Nobody asks "will you avoid asking me for anything?", yet that is required.
- States alone cannot be checked, since a reader's mind is unobservable.
- The state is needed to derive requirements and to judge what is unnecessary. The requirements are needed to check.

**Killed: "the need is just the request plus genre conventions".** In September, both the request and the conventions were satisfied, and the message still failed. The real state came from the standing arrangement.

**Killed: "the need belongs to the situation, not a party".** It cannot be checked except through a party who can act. It is kept as a "so that" clause.

**Killed: a threshold inside every state ("how sure must the decision be").** That is over-definition. State a threshold only when a source gives one.

**Refined: the implied-state diagnostic.** A model asked "what would this draft achieve?" answers fluently and wrongly. It survives only in the quoted, step-by-step form.

**Refined: conditions from the act's point.**
- Speech-act theory gives the core, but real norms vary by culture and relationship.
- So the core comes from the act, and the local conditions must be sourced.

**Survived:**
- **The three roles:** each prevents a seen failure:
  - one unnamed audience;
  - a sender-set purpose overriding the arrangement;
  - leaked or committing content.
- **The quotable-convention rule:** it handles unknown readers without personas.
- **"Constraints are never layered":** it handles a secondary but critical party.

## Open Questions

### Monitoring

In the genre test, record any requirement that is none of question, condition or constraint. Two of the same kind re-open the requirement kinds.

### Research Frontiers

- Does the cost of missing the state change how much must be explicit?
- How should exploratory exchanges, where the state is discovered as they go, be handled beyond turn by turn?
- Aesthetic communication is outside work scope.

### Refinement Triggers

- **Re-open "the need belongs to a party"** if a real case has a need that no party can be named for, even with a "so that" clause.
- **Re-open "conditions come from the act's point"** if two or more genre-test conditions can't be traced to either the act or a local source.
- **Re-open the sourcing order** if a quotable convention conflicts with an explicit request in a real case and the order gives the wrong answer.

## Source Input

<details>
<summary>Raw user input for this finding</summary>

```text
1. The need: what a communication must achieve, and for whom.
- Our current definition is "the recipient's next act → the questions it raises". That works for briefings and decisions.
- It doesn't cover:
  - several parties wanting different things;
  - unknown or later readers (documentation);
  - purposes that aren't acts (thanks, condolence, keeping a relationship).
- Core to define first: what one unit of need is. Either a question to answer, or a state the recipient should reach.
let's dive deep into this one and let's not over define it as well.
```

</details>

# [6] Finding: Several parties — holders, placement and constraints

**Source:** `/Users/nsstorm/Desktop/projects/AIOpsMan/devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_16-28__several-parties-holders-layering-and-constraints/finding.md`

---
status: active
model: claude-opus-5-5
effort: unknown
refines: devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_16-18__the-need-what-a-communication-must-achieve-and-for-whom/finding.md
---
# Finding: Several parties — holders, placement and constraints

## Changes from Prior

**Prior path:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_16-18__the-need-what-a-communication-must-achieve-and-for-whom/finding.md

**Revision trigger:** The user asked to look closely at the prior finding's three claims about communication with several parties:
1. each holder gets their own state;
2. the primary holder's requirements go in the main message and other holders' requirements go in Supporting Context;
3. constraints are never moved into Supporting Context.

**What's preserved:**
- One state per holder.
- The three roles: holder, setter, affected.
- The three ways to handle conflict: layer, split, record.

**What's changed:**
- **Claim 2 is replaced.** The new rule is placement by each holder's stopping point, plus a way in for every holder. Supporting Context serves a secondary holder only if that holder can reach it and open it.
- **Claim 3 is refined.** It now separates where a constraint holds (its scope) from where it is written (its statement). It also separates constraints on the text from constraints on a holder.

**What's new:**
- One unifying principle.
- An opening block for messages with several holders.
- Shared-core ordering.
- Plausible reach.
- Three tests, each grounded in what a reader actually sees.

**Migration:** none. Nothing implements the prior claims yet.

## Question

The prior finding said that when a communication serves several parties:
- each holder (each party it must serve) gets their own state;
- the primary holder's requirements go in the main message;
- other holders' requirements go in Supporting Context, Workcraft's linked explanations and evidence;
- constraints are never moved there.

The user asked to understand this better: whether it holds, what it means, and where it breaks. The answer had to be generic and plain, and it had to avoid over-definition.

## Finding Summary

- **Claim 1 holds, with two additions.**
  - Holders may share one state.
  - Some holders are reached *through* another holder, as when a manager briefs an executive. Then the primary holder's text must survive being forwarded.
- **Claim 2 was too simple, for three reasons.**
  - **Supporting Context was built for a different job.** Its guide is "addressed to the human or AI coordinating the work". It gives *one* recipient optional depth; it is not a place to park other people's needs.
  - **"Layering" mixes three operations that fail differently:**
    - order (what comes first);
    - access (what is visible without a click);
    - selection (what is in the message at all).
  - **Concrete failures:**
    - a cc'd team whose Friday action sits in a link never sees it;
    - a customer cannot open internal links.
- **The real driver is where each holder stops reading.** The replacement rule:
  - every holder gets **a way in from the top**: a line saying what their part is and where it is;
  - each holder's essentials come **before that holder's likely stopping point**;
  - nothing a holder reads before they stop may **mislead** them.
- **Stopping points are sourced, not guessed.** They come from the holder's role (cc means "for information"), the request, the medium, and the holder type. When none of these settles it, assume the holder stops early. That costs one pointer line.
- **Rank decides how much of each holder's material goes up top, never whether a holder is acknowledged.** Rank is set by whoever sets the communication's purpose, using stakes as evidence. The To-line is only the default.
- **Supporting Context remains valid for a secondary holder when three conditions hold:**
  - they have a way in to it;
  - they can open it;
  - what they read before reaching it doesn't mislead them.
- **Claim 3 merged two different kinds of constraint.**
  - **Constraints on the text**, such as "don't reveal X" or "don't commit the sender".
    - They hold across the communication's whole **reach**: the message, its links, plausible forwards, and summaries.
    - They usually need no statement to the reader. They do need to be stated to whoever drafts the text; an AI can't respect "don't reveal X" unless X is named in its brief.
    - "Never moved" is right about their *scope*.
  - **Constraints on a holder**, such as "embargo until Monday" or "don't share outside the team".
    - These are really requirements that the holder must know.
    - They must be stated **before the point where that holder could act**.
  - A legal footer is a known weak spot: its scope is the whole document, but its statement sits at the bottom.
- **The type of conflict decides whether to layer or split:**

| Conflict | Example | Handling |
|---|---|---|
| content | detail vs brevity | layer |
| act | approve vs be informed | layer, with an action/information line at the top |
| visibility | one holder must not see something | split |
| register | warm note vs formal record | split |

  When splitting, the facts in the shared core must match across the versions.
- **One principle covers all of it:** *no holder is stranded or misled, wherever they stop reading and wherever the communication travels.*
- **Three cheap tests, each grounded in what a reader actually sees:**
  1. Remove the links: is every holder still served?
  2. Read only the top: is anyone misled?
  3. Forward it cold: does it still make sense?
- **Confidence limit:** the cases used here were constructed. The next step is to apply the three tests to real messages that have several holders.

## Finding

### 1. Why look closer

Many work messages serve more than one party:
- a manager plus a cc'd team;
- a reviewer now and a maintainer later;
- an announcement read by customers, staff and executives.

The prior finding answered this with one rule: the primary holder in the main message, the others in Supporting Context, constraints never moved. That rule would become part of Workcraft's guidance, so it needed testing first.

### 2. Claim 1: a state per holder

This holds. Two additions come from the cases.

- **Shared states are fine.** A team announcement may give everyone the same state. "A state per holder" doesn't mean a different state for each one.
- **Serving through vs serving alongside.**
  - The September manager had to "brief others", so the answers had to "stand on their own" (from devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_12-53__what-determines-the-view-combination-an-exchange-needs/finding.md).
  - When a holder is reached through another holder, their needs become a condition on the primary holder's text: it must survive being forwarded or quoted.
  - That is a different relation from holders who read the same message side by side.

### 3. Claim 2: why "primary in main, others in Supporting Context" breaks

**Supporting Context was designed for one recipient.**
- The guide (`artifacts/skills/orchestrator-inbound-communication/references/supporting-context.md`) opens: *"Use this guide when composing, critiquing or revising a contribution addressed to the human or AI coordinating the work."*
- Its job is optional depth ("how do you know?", "more detail") for that one reader.
- Reusing it for other holders is a new use, and nobody tested it.

**"Layering" hides three operations:**

| Operation | What it decides | How it fails a secondary holder |
|---|---|---|
| Order | what comes first | They stop reading before their part. |
| Access | what is visible without a click | They never click. |
| Selection | what is in the message at all | Their part is not there. |

**The guide's own rules show the breaks:**
- **I6** counts as a problem *"an important source is inaccessible to the recipient"*. A customer cannot open an internal link, so their part cannot live there.
- **I3** requires that *"each reading level supplies faithful meaning … at the points where a reader may stop"*. With several holders there are several stopping points, and what a holder reads before theirs must not mislead them.

### 4. The replacement: placement by stopping point

Layering exists because readers stop: humans skim, executives read the top, cc'd readers read less. So the rule is placement relative to each holder's stopping point.

- **A way in for every holder.** The top tells each holder what their part is and where it is. Example: "Team: one change for you, §3, action by Friday."
  - Rank decides how much of a holder's material sits up top.
  - A secondary holder may get only this line, but never nothing.
- **Essentials before the stop.** Each holder's essential requirements sit before that holder's likely stopping point.
- **No misleading prefixes.** Whatever any holder reads before stopping must not mislead them. This is the guide's I3, applied across holders. It is the same principle as journalism's rule that every prefix of a story must stand alone, because editors cut from the bottom.

**Where stopping points come from.** They come from sources, not personality:
- the holder's role: cc and "information addressee" mean read less;
- the request: "one paragraph please";
- the medium: a chat preview vs a document;
- the holder type: an AI agent reads everything, but needs labels so it doesn't act on other holders' parts.

When nothing settles it, assume early stopping for every non-primary holder. The cost is one pointer line.

**What makes a holder primary.**
- The setter (whoever sets what the communication is for) decides.
- Stakes are the main evidence.
- The To-line is the default when nothing says otherwise.
- Reading order follows from rank. It is not a source of rank.

**Later readers.** For people who find the document later, "the top" is often its title and first line as they appear in search results. Titles carry the way in for later readers.

**Ordering by what is shared.**
- A useful default:
  1. the shared core: what every holder needs;
  2. each holder's part, labelled;
  3. depth and links.
- **Exception:** when the primary holder's action is the whole point (a decision request only they act on), their part leads.
- **Example:** in a PR description, the shared core is what changed and why. The reviewer's part is risk and testing. The maintainer's part is the alternatives that were rejected.

**An opening block, when there is more than one holder.**
- One line per holder: their part, whether it is for action or for information, and any constraint on them.
- Military and government memos separate "action addressees" from "information addressees" in the same way.
- With a single holder this reduces to stating the bottom line first. It is a pattern, not a form.

### 5. Claim 3: two kinds of constraint

**Constraints on the text: what the communication must not contain or do.**
- Examples: don't reveal X, don't commit the sender, no blame.
- **Scope:** the communication's plausible reach.
  - The message itself.
  - Everything it links to: linking to a document that reveals X reveals X.
  - Plausible forwards, judged by the medium and the sensitivity.
  - Summaries.
- **Statement:** usually no statement to the reader is needed; you simply comply. They must, however, be stated to the drafter, especially an AI drafter, which can only avoid what its brief names.
- **"Never moved" is right about their scope, and the scope is wider than the prior finding said.**

**Constraints on a holder: what a reader must not do.**
- Examples: embargo until Monday, don't share outside the team, don't act before approval.
- These are requirements that the holder must know.
- **Placement:** before the point where that holder could act, usually in the opening block.
- A legal disclaimer in a footer has whole-document scope but a low statement. It works legally, and fails communicatively whenever the reader must act on it.

### 6. When to layer, split or record

| Conflict | Example | Handling |
|---|---|---|
| Content | detail vs brevity | Layer by stopping point. |
| Act | one approves, others are informed | Layer, with action vs information marked in the opening block. |
| Visibility | one holder must not see what another must | **Split.** Layering cannot hide content from someone who receives the message. |
| Register | a warm note to a family vs a factual record for HR | **Split.** |
| Unresolvable | | **Record** the conflict openly. |

**Always splitting was rejected.** Separate messages drift apart and multiply work, and the holders lose shared awareness. When you do split, keep a shared core whose facts match. The wording may adapt to the register.

### 7. One principle and three tests

**The principle:** *no holder is stranded or misled, wherever they stop reading and wherever the communication travels.*

**The tests, each grounded in what a reader actually sees:**
1. **Remove the links.** Is every holder still served by what remains?
2. **Read only the top.** Is any holder misled, or unaware of their part?
3. **Forward it cold.** Does it make sense to someone without the original framing?

## Inherited Commitments Re-test

### Commitment: a state per holder

- **Source:** 16-18, section 4.
- **Status:** RE-TESTED. Confirmed, with the frame revised.
- **Evidence:** shared states are allowed; holders reached through another holder are added.

### Commitment: primary in main, others in Supporting Context

- **Source:** 16-18, sections 4 and 7.
- **Status:** RE-TESTED. Found INVALID as stated.
- **Evidence:**
  - The guide's addressee line, its I3 and its I6, all quoted above.
  - The cc-team and external-customer cases.
- **Replaced by:** placement by stopping point, a way in for every holder, and Supporting Context only when reachable and accessible.

### Commitment: constraints never layered

- **Source:** 16-18, sections 4 and 7.
- **Status:** RE-TESTED. Confirmed, with the frame revised.
- **Evidence:**
  - It is true for the *scope* of constraints on the text.
  - Constraints on a holder are requirements placed before action.
  - The footer-disclaimer and confidentiality-banner cases show the difference between where a constraint holds and where it is written.

### Commitment: action questions in the main message, deeper "how do you know" in Supporting Context

- **Source:** 12-53, section 5.
- **Status:** RE-TESTED. Confirmed.
- **Evidence:** this is the single-holder case of placement by stopping point. Supporting Context remains depth for a holder who can reach it.

### Commitment: Supporting Context guide I1–I7

- **Source:** `artifacts/skills/orchestrator-inbound-communication/references/supporting-context.md`
- **Status:** RE-TESTED. Confirmed, with the frame revised.
- **Evidence:**
  - I3 and I6 are correct, and they generalise per holder.
  - The guide's scope (one orchestrator) is the part that needs extending.

## Next Actions

### MUST

- **What:** Apply the three tests (links removed, top only, forwarded cold) to 4 real messages with several holders: a cc email, a PR description, an announcement, and a decision record.
- **Who:** a test inquiry; the user picks the texts.
- **Gate:** before changing the Supporting Context guide.
- **Why:** so far the model rests on constructed cases.

### COULD

- **What:** Extend the Supporting Context guide from one recipient to several holders:
  - per-holder reading levels (I3);
  - per-holder access (I6);
  - a way-in line per holder.
- **Who:** the guide's maintainer.
- **Gate:** after the MUST.
- **Why:** the guide would then be valid for messages with several holders.
- **Depends-on:** the MUST "apply the three tests". This COULD is GATED.

### DEFERRED

- **What:** A per-holder fit check, and using a holder's follow-up questions as evidence of where they stopped.
  - **Gate:** when the fit checks are built.
  - **Why (if revived):** it would make stopping points observable.
- **What:** Writing so AI summaries keep the essentials and the constraints on holders.
  - **Gate:** when an AI-summarised channel is in use.

## Reasoning

**Killed: always split.**
- It removes every layering problem, but messages drift apart, work multiplies, and holders lose shared awareness.
- What survived from it: the shared core used when you do split.

**Replaced: "others in Supporting Context".**
- The guide was built for one recipient.
- Its own I6 (access) and I3 (reading levels) show the failures.

**Refined:**
- **"Constraints never layered"** is now scope vs statement, and text vs holder constraints. A footer disclaimer and a confidential banner are both constraints placed in a layer, and both behave as predicted.
- **"Shared core first"** gets an exception: the primary leads when their action is the point.
- **"Shared core word-for-word identical"** becomes the facts match. Registers legitimately differ.

**Survived:**
- placement by stopping point;
- the unifying principle;
- plausible reach;
- the opening block as a pattern;
- labels for AI holders.

Two pieces of established practice support this:
- military and government memos separate action addressees from information addressees;
- journalism holds that every prefix of a story must stand alone.

## Open Questions

### Monitoring

In the MUST test, record any holder who is failed despite passing all three tests. Two such cases re-open the model.

### Research Frontiers

- Can a holder's follow-up questions reliably show where they stopped?
- How do live threads work, where holders change turn by turn?
- Do AI summaries become the real stopping point for many readers?

### Refinement Triggers

- **Re-open "assume early stopping by default"** if, in real texts, the pointer lines clutter messages without helping any holder.
- **Re-open the reach bound** if a real leak happens through a link or forward the "plausible" judgment excluded.

## Source Input

<details>
<summary>Raw user input for this finding</summary>

```text
- Several parties: each holder gets its own state.
  - The primary holder's requirements go in the main message. Other holders' requirements go in Supporting Context (Workcraft's linked explanations and evidence).
  - Constraints always apply to the whole message. They are never moved into Supporting Context. dive deep into this like this try to understand this better.
```

</details>
