# Finding: what the base reference for texts holds, built from this project alone

## Changes from Prior

**Prior paths:**
- **This inquiry's first iteration:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_13-28__something-is-off-base-references-and-workspace-alignment/docarchive/finding_iter1.md (called "iteration 1" below). It answered that "something is off" is a comparison, and alignment, against what every text of its kind should be. It said those expectations belong, written down, in a "base reference" in the workspace.
- **The implicit-contracts finding:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_14-41__implicit-contracts-explicit-references-and-aligncraft/finding.md (called 14-41 below). It named unwritten expectations an implicit contract. It set the rule for which expectations must be written, and proposed an AlignCraft chapter.

**Revision trigger: your correction, given with this resume.**
> "this is a standalone seperate project from storyos or stormideas etc, dont refer to local different prjects or their skills etc.."

- Iteration 1 had filled the base reference by pointing to pattern catalogues and preference lists from other local projects. 14-41 repeated that list.
- **That material is removed. Nothing in this finding comes from it.**
- This iteration rebuilt the base reference from this project's own material only:
  - Communication Craft's findings;
  - its published explicitness bank and skills;
  - AlignCraft;
  - outside public sources.

**What is preserved:**
- **From iteration 1:**
  - "something is off" is a comparison and alignment;
  - two scopes of what was meant;
  - the base reference lives in the workspace;
  - it is not a seventh layer;
  - one reference for the writer and the checker;
  - the growth loop;
  - "accountable, not automatic".
- **From 14-41:**
  - the three grounds for leaving an expectation unwritten;
  - high-stakes expectations are always written;
  - competence stays implicit;
  - the AlignCraft chapter and its four edits.

**What has changed:**
- **The base reference's contents.** Iteration 1's mapping onto other projects' material is replaced by a structure built here (sections 3–9).
- **The word "standing".** In Communication Craft it is replaced by your own words (section 13), because this project already uses "standing" for something else.
- **The AlignCraft answer.** Unchanged in substance. 14-41's list of base-reference contents (its section 8) is corrected by a dated note.
- **MUST/COULD drift against iteration 1.** Items from other local projects are shown as "[removed]", by your instruction.
  - **The index.**
    - Prior MUST: "Write the base reference index, linking to the documents and copying nothing. The standing core: the bank; [removed]; readable form (new); harmony's rules (new). The kind-specific parts: [removed]".
    - New MUST: "Write the base reference document: three principles and their rules, the kind parts for the substrate and alignment documents, the workspace's style sheet, cited standards, and how it works."
    - Rationale: the standalone correction, and 14-41's rule for what must be written.
  - **The missing rules.**
    - Prior COULD: "Draft the missing rules (readable form, paragraph complementarity, harmony's kinds) … and test them on the benchmarks before adopting any".
    - New MUST: "Test the drafted rules … before adopting any".
    - Rationale: the drafts now exist (section 5). Testing them is what gives the base reference its evidence.
  - **Two COULDs dropped:**
    - "Add one sentence to the catalogues' basis rule …";
    - "Check that every catalogue rule declares its basis …".

    Rationale: both concern other projects' catalogues.
  - **ISO 24495-1.**
    - Prior COULD: "Compare ISO 24495-1's four principles with the project's principles, as a candidate principle level".
    - Now answered (section 3): it is a cited standard, not the principle level.
  - **AlignCraft.**
    - Prior MUST: "Decide on AlignCraft: option (b), (a) or (c), or no change."
    - Superseded by 14-41's MUST (decide on its chapter). This finding adds nothing to that chapter.

**What is new:**
- three principles, set by what each check compares the text with;
- your three references placed inside the first;
- drafted rules for consistency, for how paragraphs complement each other, and for readable form, each with its reason and exceptions;
- one exception principle, with a strict showing;
- precedence written into the base reference;
- the workspace's part as a style sheet of the owner's decisions;
- test texts for rules;
- two ways of adopting a rule.

**Migration:**
- dated notes appended to 14-41, 11-29 and 09-00;
- the memory note on communication alignment updated.

Both are done at conclusion.

## Question

**This inquiry's question,** close to your words in its Source Input:
- "noticing something is off" is still a comparison "with the expected integrity, with the expected quality";
- so there should be written "base references" to compare with:
  - "textual integrity";
  - "multiple paragraph text quality";
  - "how paragraphs should be complementing each other";
- then "something is off" "has a place of comparison", "it doesn't live in our head", and it "will be an alignment with the workspace".

**This iteration's focus,** after 14-41 and your standalone correction: what does the base reference hold?
- its principle level;
- which of this project's written rules belong in it, and which expectations may stay unwritten, with evidence;
- the missing parts, as rules with reasons and exceptions;
- the part the workspace adds for itself;
- how it is enforced and kept current;
- what AlignCraft should say.

## Finding Summary

**The answer, in one line**
- **It holds, written down, what every text of its kind should be:**
  - your textual-integrity rules, including how paragraphs agree with and complement each other;
  - the rules for being faithful and for fitting the exchange;
  - the rules for each kind of text;
  - your workspace's own decisions.

  It never holds what one particular text was meant to say. That stays in the substrate, the request and the sources.

**Its top: three principles, set by what each check compares the text with**
- **Textual integrity:** the text can be read as meant from the text and its stated setting alone. Three parts:
  - determinate (it singles out what its wording needs);
  - consistent (its parts belong together);
  - readable form (the medium can show it).
- **Faithful:** it says only what its sources support and its author authorised, and keeps what earlier versions settled. Compared with the sources, the author's brief and earlier versions.
- **Fit:** it gives this exchange what it needs. Compared with the request, the template for its kind of text, and the stated relationship.

**Your three references, placed**
- **Textual integrity** is the first principle.
- **Multi-paragraph text quality** is "consistent" applied to the whole text: the meanings agree, they bear on one point, the telling follows a path, and the emphasis matches what matters. It also includes the structure a kind of text requires.
- **How paragraphs complement each other:**
  - each part bears on the point;
  - each does a job no other part does, unless the text says it is a summary or recap;
  - parts set side by side answer the same kind of question;
  - their relations are stated.
- **"Nothing missing" is separate.** It belongs to "fit", checked against the template for the kind of text.

**What exists, and what is new**
- **"Determinate" is already written and tested:** this project's explicitness bank. 79–82% of its reported gaps were real, against 65% for the AI without the rules.
- **The rules for "consistent", "complementing" and "readable form" are now drafted,** each with its reason and its exceptions. They are drafts until tested.

**How a rule is written**
- **The rule, why it matters, and its valid exceptions:** AlignCraft's own ground-rule format. What it is compared with, when it applies, and its status are stated once per group.
- **A rule that claims to find problems also carries a test pair:** a text that breaks it and a text that meets it. The pair comes first from the correction that produced the rule.
- **A rule whose only test is the text's purpose is not a detection rule.** That judgment belongs to whoever holds the purpose.

**Exceptions: one principle**
- **A deviation counts as valid only where the rule's reason does not hold here.** It must be shown, in one of three ways:
  - quote what supplies the meaning;
  - show each alternative with its own result, all the same;
  - quote where the text says so.
- **"Following the rule would make this text worse" is decided by whoever holds the text's purpose.** The author decides when writing their own text. An AI drafter or checker asks.
- **This project measured why the showing must be strict.** A loose showing let wrong exemptions through. A strict one fixed that, but did not find more gaps.

**The parts, and who owns them**
- **Every text:** the three principles and their rules. Owned by the framework's author.
- **Each kind of text:** its template, how strict it is, its structure. First candidates: the substrate, and alignment documents.
- **Your workspace:** a style sheet of your decisions, in six slots:
  - terms;
  - style choices;
  - your kinds of text and their templates;
  - what texts in your name may or may not offer, promise or decide;
  - cited standards and their order;
  - deliberate departures, with reasons.
- **Cited standards:** pointers, never copies.

**Precedence, written in**
- **Reading, faithfulness and high-stakes rules are floors.** A kind of text or high stakes can tighten them. A workspace cannot lower them.
- **Conventions:** the more local wins, applied consistently.
- **Your workspace's terms supply a meaning only for readers the setting says share them.**

**What is written, and what may stay unwritten**
- **Written:** everything someone will hold a text to, unless it is shared, makes no difference, or is already said by a cited standard (14-41's rule).
- **Grammar and standard spelling, clear sentences and sound reasoning stay unwritten.** Grammar and spelling are shared, or covered by a cited dictionary or grammar. Clear sentences and sound reasoning are competence.
- **Only explicitness is measured as something the AI does not already do on its own.** Every other "shared with the AI" claim needs a check first.

**How it stays current**
- **Conventions are adopted on your word.** They are decisions, not claims.
- **Rules that claim to find problems are adopted only after a test.** This project measured that "Rules buy precision and cost recall".
- **The same correction twice → propose a rule, with its test pair.** A rule that causes friction is refined or removed.
- **Re-check "shared" after each model change.**

**AlignCraft**
- **14-41's chapter "Implicit Contracts" and its four edits stand unchanged.** Nothing is added.
- **AlignCraft already has the layering:** "Universal Rules: Apply to every project, every time" and "Project Preferences: Specific to your tech stack, team, or domain". The base reference for texts mirrors it.

## Finding

### 1. Why this question

**Iteration 1 answered the classification.** "Something is off" is a comparison, and alignment, against what every text of its kind should be. Those expectations should be written in a "base reference" in the workspace.

**14-41 then set the rule for which expectations must be written.** Every one someone will hold the work to, unless:
- **shared:** everyone involved, the AI included, already does it the same way;
- **no difference:** any reasonable way of meeting it gives the same result;
- **declared elsewhere:** a standard you point to already says it.

**Neither answered what the base reference actually holds without leaning on other projects' material.** You ruled that out. So this iteration built it from:
- **this project's Communication Craft findings:**
  - textual integrity (11-29);
  - harmony's kinds (devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_07-57__content-and-substrate-harmony/finding.md, called 07-57);
  - what a text may leave unsaid (devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-09_16-52__conveyance-what-a-text-may-leave-unsaid/finding.md, called the conveyance finding);
  - the need (devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_16-18__the-need-what-a-communication-must-achieve-and-for-whom/finding.md);
  - the peer properties of a text (compare/devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-05_18-49__peer-categories-of-explicitness-in-communication-problems/finding.md, called the peer taxonomy);
- **its published explicitness bank and skills** (public in the workcraft repository);
- **AlignCraft;**
- **outside public sources.**

### 2. What the base reference is

**It is the workspace's written ground rules for texts:** "what every text of its kind should be", written down. While unwritten, the same expectations are the implicit contract 14-41 described. Written, they are the explicit one.

**It is an AlignCraft Level 0 artifact.** The match is by what it does, not by a shared word:
- AlignCraft's Level 0 artifacts are "convention guides" that lock down "The environment and its rules";
- Level 0 asks "What conventions are in place" and "What constraints apply (technical, organizational, stylistic)".

So AlignCraft's own criteria for an aligned artifact apply to the base reference:
- it "Captures a decision";
- it "Has a clear level";
- it "Is verifiable";
- it "Is current".

**It holds rules only.**
- What one particular text was meant to say lives in the substrate (the written account of what a message must achieve and say), the request, the sources and earlier versions.
- Each rule names what it is compared with, so a check knows which of those inputs it needs.

**It is a text itself.** It must meet its own rules.

### 3. Its principle level: three principles, set by input

**The axis is what each check must compare the text with.** This project's peer taxonomy defines every property of a text as "one property, checked against one thing, one test question". It groups the properties into three families by that input. The base reference takes the same three as its principles:

| Principle | Compared with | The principle | Its rule groups (the project's 15 properties of a text) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Textual integrity** | the text and its stated setting | The text can be read as meant from the text and its stated setting alone. It is **determinate** (where the wording needs one particular thing, the text singles it out), **consistent** (its parts belong together as one) and in **readable form** (the stated medium can show it). | explicitness; ambiguity (two readings); vagueness; connection; order; consistency; implication |
| **Faithful** | the sources, the author's brief or statement, earlier versions | The text says only what its sources support and its author authorised, and keeps what earlier versions settled. | support; authority; preservation |
| **Fit** | the request, the template for its kind of text, the stated relationship | The text gives this exchange what it needs. It answers what was asked, holds what its kind of text requires, carries nothing that burdens without serving the purpose, and speaks as the relationship calls for. | responsiveness; completeness for purpose; economy; register; processability |

**Why this axis and not a published one.** ISO 24495-1, the plain-language standard, has four principles: readers get what they need, can find it, can understand it, and can use it. They describe what readers achieve, which is tested with readers. This project judges a text from the text and its stated setting, never from what a reader knows. ISO's set also has no principle about being true to sources.

So ISO 24495-1 is a **cited standard** inside the base reference, for its guidelines. It does not organise the base reference. The same holds for:
- **Grice's cooperative principle:** "Make your contribution such as is required, at the stage at which it occurs, by the accepted purpose or direction of the talk exchange";
- **de Beaugrande and Dressler's standards of textuality.**

### 4. Your three references, placed

**All three are judged on the same input,** the text and its stated setting. So they are parts of one principle, textual integrity, not three separate references with their own principles. Your names are kept for the parts:

| Your reference | Where it sits | What holds it |
|---|---|---|
| **Textual integrity** | the first principle | determinate, consistent, readable form |
| **Multiple paragraph text quality** | "consistent", applied to the whole text, plus the structure a kind of text requires | the consistency rules (section 5.2), and the kind's part (section 8) |
| **How paragraphs should be complementing each other** | the part of "consistent" about how parts divide and join the work | the complementing rules (section 5.3), plus "every part bears on the point" (section 5.2) |

**"Nothing missing" is not part of complementing.** 07-57 says content harmony is "Not completeness". A text can hold together and still lack its ask. Whether every needed part is present is a fit rule, checked against the template for its kind of text.

**One edge is uncertain:** whether you meant "multiple paragraph text quality" more widely, to include structure and findability. The table covers both readings, so it does not narrow your meaning.

**The earlier examples sort cleanly:**
- **"the change" when two changes are in play** is a determinacy gap. The bank decides it.
- **Two sentences that contradict each other** break "meanings agree" (section 5.2).

### 5. The rules under textual integrity

These are the "missing contract parts" for texts. **Every rule here is compared with the text and its stated setting,** unless the table says otherwise. **Every rule is a draft until tested,** unless marked tested.

#### 5.1 Determinate: written and tested

- **The rule:** where the wording needs one particular thing (which one, of what, who, what is done, by what criterion, when, where), the text singles it out at or before that point.
- **Its exceptions:** the conveyance finding's three grounds:
  - the text or its stated setting supplies it;
  - any reasonable choice gives the same result;
  - the text says it is open, or states a level of detail.
- **Its checker's form:** the explicitness bank (version 6.7.2, public). Its own rule: "The text is the setting plus the passage."
- **Status: tested.**
  - 79–82% of its reported gaps were judged real, against 65% for the same AI with no rules.
  - Gaps found by two independent runs were real 85% of the time.

#### 5.2 Consistent: the whole text belongs together (drafts)

These come from 07-57's account of how the meanings of a text hold together. Each is tested first on 07-57's own constructed cases.

| Rule | Why it matters | Valid exceptions |
|---|---|---|
| **Meanings agree.** No two places give incompatible facts, figures, statuses, claims or decisions. | The reader cannot take one account ("Wait, which is it?"). An AI reader resolves it silently. | **The text says so:** a stated disagreement, a precedence ("clause 9 prevails"), options side by side. **A limit elsewhere in the text reconciles them:** restore the limit first; the gap is then a determinacy gap. **No difference:** values within the text's stated precision ("about 10k" and "9,850"). |
| **One name per thing,** unless the text ties a new name to the old. | A renamed thing is taken as a new thing. | **The text says so:** "X (from here on, Y)". **The workspace's terms supply it** (section 9). |
| **Every part bears on the point.** | A part that relates to nothing makes the reader hunt for its purpose ("What does this have to do with the rest?"). AI drafters add parts that have no home. | **The text says so:** a marked aside, an appendix. **Separate notices in one message need no link.** |
| **The parts establish one point,** or the text says it has several and keeps them apart. | 07-57: the meanings "establish one thing". Minto: "Ideas at any level in the pyramid must always be summaries of the ideas grouped below them". | **The text says so:** "three updates". **Kinds that are plural by nature:** a glossary, a reference list. |
| **No claim is stronger than the support the text itself gives,** unless the text points to support outside it. | An overreach misleads: "a conclusion stronger than the evidence stated in the same text". | **The text says so:** "we expect", "in our view". **A cited source:** then the faithful principle applies instead. |
| **The telling follows a path.** Each part follows from what came before or says how it connects. A topic is told in one place, and not reopened without saying why. | The reader loses the thread ("why are we back here?"). | **The text says so:** a recap, "as noted above". **Kinds with a fixed order:** forms, reference pages. A meaning given only after the point that needs it is a determinacy gap, not this rule. **On watch:** 07-57 found no real text yet where the path fails on its own, so this rule may merge into the order property. |
| **The emphasis matches what the text says matters most;** the main point is not buried. | Readers take position, space and marking as importance. | **The text says so:** "in date order". **Kinds whose order is set:** logs, timelines. |
| **One speaker and one audience,** unless the text marks a change. | A shift changes who commits and who is asked. | **The text says so:** a quotation; a part addressed by name to another group. |

**Surface changes in register, terms or format are sorted, not ruled.** 11-29's substitution test asks whether a reader with only the text and its setting would take a different meaning.
- **If yes,** it is a breach of "one name per thing" or of "one speaker and one audience".
- **If no,** it is a style matter for the workspace's style choices.

A separate rule, "change the surface only where it serves the whole", was drafted and dropped. It could only be decided by the text's purpose, so it detected nothing (section 6).

#### 5.3 Complementing: how parts divide and join the work (drafts)

"Every part bears on the point" (above) belongs here too. It is not repeated.

| Rule | Why it matters | Valid exceptions |
|---|---|---|
| **Each part does a job no other part does;** a part that repeats another is cut. | A repeat burdens the reader without serving. Two places stating the same thing can drift into disagreement. | **The text says so:** a summary, a recap, an executive summary, "to confirm:". **It does a job:** repetition the stakes call for, such as confirming a time zone or an amount. The conveyance finding: deliberate redundancy "is not indifferent at fine grain". |
| **Parts set side by side** (sections, list items, columns) **are of the same kind and answer the same question,** or the text says how they differ. | Minto: "Ideas in each grouping must always be the same kind of idea". The peer taxonomy's connection property: "which question each parallel point answers". | **The text says so:** a labelled mixed list. |
| **Where the point rests on how two parts relate** (cause, support, contrast, condition, sequence), **the text states the relation,** or a cue makes it plain. | The reader is left asking "Why? So what? How does this relate?" | **No difference:** the relation changes nothing for the point. **Separate notices need no link.** **Status: partly tested.** The bank already checks relations within the text where the text gives a cue. This rule is a draft only for relations across distant parts. |

#### 5.4 Readable form

| Rule | Why it matters | Valid exceptions | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| **The text uses only the formatting its stated medium shows:** markup, tables, lists, links, length. | Unshown markup, or a table in a plain-text channel, garbles the reading. 11-29: "Pure form failures need no one's meaning", so the fix needs no author. | **No difference:** markup that still reads as plain text, such as a dash list. | draft. Compared with the text and the stated medium. With no medium stated, the kind's default medium applies; if none, the check asks. |
| **A structure the content has** (steps in order, a comparison across items) **is shown as a list or table where the medium shows them.** | A sequence or comparison is taken in one pass only when laid out. | **The medium cannot show it** (use numbered sentences). **Kinds where prose is expected.** | **deferred.** It may be competence ("how to write a clear sentence"). It is written only if the same layout correction recurs twice. |

#### 5.5 The other two principles

- **Faithful's rule groups:**
  - support: every claim of fact, status or result is within what the sources support;
  - authority: every commitment in the author's name is authorised;
  - preservation: meaning survives revision, and removed or corrected content stays out.
- **Fit's rule groups:**
  - responsiveness: the main line answers what was asked;
  - completeness for purpose: the text holds what its kind's template requires;
  - economy: nothing burdens without serving the purpose;
  - register: voice and stance suit the stated relationship.
  - Processability, being readable in one pass, is competence until corrections show otherwise.

These are named here and drafted later. Your three references sit in the first principle.

### 6. How a rule is written

**Each rule states three things,** AlignCraft's own ground-rule format:
- the rule;
- why it matters;
- its valid exceptions.

**Three more things are stated once, for the principle or group, and inherited:**
- what it is compared with;
- when it applies (the kind of text, the medium, the stakes);
- its status: tested, draft, on watch, or deferred.

This matches what this project's overview asks of every check: "we need to know when it applies, what it examines and what evidence supports its judgment".

**A rule that claims to find problems carries a test pair:** one text that breaks it and one that meets it.
- **The pair makes the rule:**
  - verifiable, AlignCraft's own criterion ("Both sides can point to it and agree or disagree");
  - testable before adoption;
  - usable in the default check (section 11);
  - teachable to an AI drafter by example.
- **This is common practice in code.** Linter rule pages show "Examples of incorrect code for this rule" and "Examples of correct code for this rule".
- **The pair is taken first from the correction that produced the rule.** The original text is the one that breaks it, and the corrected text the one that meets it.
- **A pair an AI writes needs the owner's confirmation.** Otherwise it would encode the AI's own defaults.
- **The pair illustrates the rule.** The rule's wording decides its edges.

**Conventions carry one example, not a pair.** A convention is a decision, such as "we spell British", and makes no claim that a breach is a real problem.

**A rule whose only test is the text's purpose is not admitted as a detection rule.** That judgment belongs to whoever holds the purpose (section 7). This is why "change the surface only where it serves the whole" was dropped.

### 7. Exceptions: one principle, shown strictly

**A deviation from a rule counts as valid only where the rule's reason does not hold here.** Three ways can be shown at detection, from the text alone:
1. **Supplied:** quote what meets the rule's purpose another way.
2. **No difference:** name each reasonable alternative with its own result, judged against the rule's own purpose, and show the results are the same.
3. **The text says so:** quote where it declares the openness, the tension, the level, the summary or the alias.

**The per-rule lists in section 5 are cues** for where to look. They do not decide on their own.

**The fourth way is "following the rule would make this text worse".** Style guides allow it: "Depart from it when doing so improves your content" (Google's developer documentation style guide), and "know when to be inconsistent" (PEP 8, the Python style guide). But it needs the text's purpose, which is what was meant for this one text. So whoever holds that purpose decides it:
- **an author writing their own text** decides it, and keeps the departure consistent through the text ("When you depart from this guide, be consistent throughout your document");
- **an AI drafter or checker** does not decide it alone. It goes to the author as a question, or is decided against what the author has already settled.

An AI that judges "departing improves this" by itself is the self-confirming loop 14-41 described: its defaults write the text and then excuse it.

**Why the showing must be strict.** This project tested this exact question on the explicitness bank:
- **The first trial allowed one shared result:** "All 229 'indifferent' exemptions gave a single shared result … Many of those results only restated the sentence." Wrong exemptions got through.
- **The strict version fixed it:** "per-filler results, the slot's own act". Result: "The leak is fixed".
- **It found no more gaps:** "The principle holds, but it is not a recall lever."

So the strict showing keeps exceptions honest and checkable. It does not make a check find more.

**Each check's findings record the exception used and its evidence.** The bank already does this: its "not demanded" answer names the test that decided it.

### 8. The parts, and who owns them

| Part | What it holds | Owner |
|---|---|---|
| **Every text** | the three principles and their rules (section 5) | the framework's author (for this project, you) |
| **Each kind of text** | its template (what it must hold); how strict it is; its required structure; which of its items are floors | the workspace, or the framework for kinds it ships |
| **The workspace** | a style sheet of the owner's decisions (below) | the workspace's owner |
| **Cited standards** | pointers to outside standards, such as ISO 24495-1, a style guide, or an accessibility standard | whoever cites them |

**This mirrors AlignCraft's ground rules,** by purpose:
- "Universal Rules: Apply to every project, every time" corresponds to the every-text part;
- "Project Preferences: Specific to your tech stack, team, or domain" corresponds to the kind and workspace parts.

**The first kind parts to write are those whose criteria are already written:**
- **the substrate:** 11-29's check that its decisions are captured, its levels marked, its entries verifiable and consistent, and that "left open on purpose" is kept apart from "not yet settled";
- **alignment documents:** AlignCraft's four criteria.

Agent prompts (which need fine grain, because AI readers fill gaps silently) and messages follow.

**How a check knows which kind part applies:** the setting states the kind and the medium. Failing that, the workspace's default for that channel applies. Failing that, the check asks.

**The workspace part is a living style sheet.** Book editing keeps two documents apart:
- a style guide "provides broad rules for an entire category of publications";
- a style sheet "records how those rules (and any deliberate departures from them) apply to your specific manuscript".

The workspace part is the second kind. Six slots:
1. **terms:** one name per thing in this workspace;
2. **style choices:** spelling variety, formats, voice;
3. **the kinds of text this workspace produces,** with their templates;
4. **standing authorisations and refusals:** what texts in the owner's name may or may not offer, promise or decide. This is the lasting part of the "authority" property;
5. **cited standards, and their order;**
6. **deliberate departures from a cited standard,** each with its reason.

**Every entry must be quotable,** as this project already requires: "A convention counts only if it can be quoted." So:
- each convention is written as the owner's decision ("our status updates lead with the decision");
- a convention is never justified as a claim about readers. "'Readers usually want…' is not a source."

**For a workspace that adopts this framework,** the every-text part is a cited standard. 14-41's three grounds then apply again at that level.

### 9. Precedence, written in

**An unstated precedence is one more implicit contract:** the AI would settle conflicts by its defaults. So the base reference states its own. Two relations exist in outside practice, for two kinds of expectation:

- **Floors:** reading rules, faithfulness rules, and items marked high-stakes.
  - The kind of text and the stakes can tighten them.
  - A text may deviate at one point by section 7.
  - A workspace cannot lower them in general.
  - The Scrum Guide has this relation: an organisation's Definition of Done is followed "as a minimum".
  - Lowering a reading rule does not change a convention. It stops the text from being read one way.
- **Overrides:** conventions (style choices, terms, templates).
  - The more local wins: the workspace over a kind part, a kind part over a cited guide.
  - Google's style guide: "1. Project-specific style. 2. This style guide. 3. Third-party references." PEP 8: "Consistency within a project is more important" than consistency with the guide.
  - Once overridden, the convention is applied consistently.

**A conflict between a rule and what was meant for this text** is decided against what the author has settled, or asked. AlignCraft's ground rules say the same: "Ask for clarification if rules conflict with requirements."

**"Floor" marks items, never whole kinds.** A kind part declares how strict it is, and marks each of its items as a floor or a convention.

**Local terms meet the floor; they do not lower it.** A team's abbreviations look like a local lowering of "determinate". They are not:
- the meaning is supplied by the workspace's terms, which count as stated shared ground, since the bank counts "the setting plus the passage";
- this holds only for texts whose setting says the reader shares those terms, and only when the terms are given to the checker as part of the setting;
- for any other reader, a client for example, the floor applies unchanged.

**This is the recipients' side of the base reference.** The reader enters only through what the setting states. No reader profile is involved.

### 10. What is written, and what may stay unwritten

**14-41's rule decides:** write every expectation someone will hold a text to, unless it is shared, makes no difference, or is said by a cited standard. High-stakes expectations are written regardless. Competence stays implicit.

**This project's written material, sorted:**

| Written item | In the base reference? | Where | Evidence that writing it matters |
|---|---|---|---|
| 11-29's textual integrity | yes | the first principle | your own reference; needs no reader knowledge |
| The explicitness bank | yes | the tested rules and checker for "determinate" | **measured:** 79–82% real gaps against 65% for the AI without the rules. So this expectation is not "shared with the AI". |
| The conveyance finding's three grounds | yes | the exception principle | **measured:** exemptions written as surface conditions let real gaps through |
| 07-57's account of harmony | yes, as draft rules | consistency and complementing | **qualitative only:** "AI drafters add parts that have no home in the substrate". The bank does not catch disagreement between distant places. |
| The peer taxonomy's 15 properties | yes, as rule groups | under the three principles | two properties are provisional (ambiguity in the strict sense, and implication) |
| "A convention counts only if it can be quoted" | yes | how the workspace part works | already a project rule |
| AlignCraft's artifact criteria; 11-29's substrate check | yes | kind parts | your own methodology |
| The fix skill's rule against adding meaning nothing establishes | its content, yes | under "faithful" | the rule itself stays in the skill |

**May stay unwritten:**

| Expectation | Ground | Evidence status |
|---|---|---|
| grammar and standard spelling | shared; also covered by a cited dictionary or grammar | not checked for the AI, low risk. The *spelling variety* is a workspace style choice. |
| clear sentences that read in one pass; sound reasoning | competence (14-41: "how to write a clear sentence") | becomes a rule only if the same correction recurs |
| choices that change nothing for whoever judges | no difference | shown case by case (section 7) |
| plain-language guidelines (headings, findability) | covered by a cited standard: ISO 24495-1 or a style guide | the standard's guidelines were not read here (paywalled) |

**"Shared with the AI" is a dated result, not an assumption.** Only explicitness is measured, and only on the checking side.

### 11. How it stays current

1. **Write the known contract parts first,** including every high-stakes one (14-41).
2. **Give them to the AI before it works, and check against the same contract.**
   - The drafter gets the principles, a summary of the rules for its kind of text, and the test pairs.
   - The checker gets the full rules, or a bank where one exists.
   - The reference and the bank are two forms of one contract. Today only "determinate" has a bank. Other drafts are applied directly.
3. **Two adoption paths, by what a rule is:**
   - **conventions are adopted on the owner's word.** They are decisions; there is nothing to measure;
   - **rules that claim to find problems are adopted only after their test pairs and a benchmark show they add real findings without false ones.** This project measured that "Rules buy precision and cost recall". One of its rules once "suppressed a real planted gap".
4. **Keep a record of judgments that fit no rule yet:** the text, the judgment, and its nearest principle.
   - When the same correction recurs twice, propose a rule, with the original and corrected texts as its test pair.
   - AlignCraft: "When you correct AI repeatedly → Add a rule". An AI may propose; the owner adopts.
5. **Refine or remove a rule that causes friction.** AlignCraft: "When rules cause friction → Refine or remove", and "When context changes → Update accordingly".
6. **Run the default check, and re-run it after each model change.** For an expectation left unwritten as "shared":
   - have the AI work without the rule on texts where the expectation applies;
   - record whether it meets it, dated.
   - **The checking side was run once,** for explicitness: the 65% against 79–82% comparison. **The drafting side has not been run.**
7. **Version the rules.** Keep layers, newest first, as the bank does. AlignCraft: "An outdated document is worse than no document."

### 12. AlignCraft

**14-41's proposal stands unchanged:**
- one general chapter, "Implicit Contracts", in Part II, before "Essential Ground Rules";
- four small edits (Level 0, Coherence, The Data Dump, and optionally Part II's description).

The chapter's examples are generic. Its line for texts already reads: "Messages and other texts: a base reference of what every text of its kind should be."

**Nothing is added.** Two additions were considered and rejected:
- **A sentence saying some expectations are floors that can be tightened but not relaxed.**
  - For texts, the floors rest on the reading.
  - For code, what protects whether the output can be used at all (correctness, security) was not examined here.
  - Stating floors in AlignCraft now would generalise without evidence. It would add "More explanation than benefit", AlignCraft's own warning.
- **Renaming "Two Types of Rules" because the chapter goes on to list domain-specific and language-specific rules.** On a close reading there is no contradiction: "Project Preferences: Specific to your tech stack, team, or domain" already covers both.

**14-41's list of the base reference's contents** (its section 8) named other projects' material. It is corrected by a dated note pointing here.

### 13. Names

- **"Base reference" and "contract parts" are kept.** They are your words.
- **In Communication Craft, "standing" is replaced by "what every text of its kind should be"** (your words), or "the contract for texts" for short.
  - This project already uses "standing" for how firmly a fact in the substrate is held, and on what basis. One word would carry two meanings.
  - In AlignCraft's chapter, "standing contract" can stay: that collision does not occur there.
  - **This is your choice.**
- **"Declared" is said three ways,** because they are different relations:
  - "the text says so" (an exception);
  - "the setting states it" (an input that selects rules);
  - "a cited standard says it" (14-41's ground for leaving something unwritten).
- **"Workspace conventions" replaces "house".** AlignCraft's own phrase is "What conventions are in place".

### 14. Outside grounding

| Source | What it says | What it supports |
|---|---|---|
| ISO 24495-1:2023 (plain language) | readers get what they need, can find it, understand it and use it | a cited standard, not the axis (section 3) |
| Grice | "Make your contribution such as is required, at the stage at which it occurs, by the accepted purpose or direction of the talk exchange"; flouting is a deliberate, open break | standing expectations of every contribution; why the purpose-holder decides departures |
| de Beaugrande and Dressler | seven standards of textuality; "if they are defied, that form of behaviour will break down" | why reading rules are floors |
| Clark and Haviland, the given-new contract | "The speaker agrees to try to construct the Given and New information of each utterance in context … so that the listener is able to compute from memory the unique Antecedent …" | a published name for a standing contract of communication. Its "from memory" is replaced here by "from the text and its stated setting". |
| Google developer documentation style guide | "Use the following references, including this guide, in this order: 1. Project-specific style. 2. This style guide. 3. Third-party references."; "This guide contains guidelines, not rules. Depart from it when doing so improves your content." | the override order; departures |
| PEP 8 | "Consistency within a project is more important. Consistency within one module or function is the most important."; "know when to be inconsistent" | local conventions win |
| Scrum Guide 2020 | the Definition of Done is "a formal description of the state of the Increment when it meets the quality measures required for the product"; an organisation's is followed "as a minimum" | floors |
| Minto, the pyramid principle | ideas above summarise the ideas grouped below; ideas in a group are "the same kind of idea"; "logically ordered" | the complementing and one-point rules |
| ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148 | separate quality characteristics for single requirements and for the whole set (complete, consistent) | sentence-level against whole-text expectations |
| Editorial style sheets | "records how those rules (and any deliberate departures from them) apply to your specific manuscript" | the workspace part |
| ESLint rule documentation | "Examples of incorrect code for this rule" / "Examples of correct code for this rule" | test pairs |
| ISO 9000 | a requirement is "stated, generally implied or obligatory" | unwritten expectations are the "implied" kind (iteration 1) |

## Reasoning

### Killed, and what each taught

- **A seventh layer, and "conformance" as a separate family** (iteration 1). They stay killed: no new evidence.
- **The mapping onto other projects' material** (iteration 1 and 14-41). Removed by your instruction.
  - **What it taught:** this project's own findings already held most of the content, as definitions waiting to be written as rules.
- **ISO 24495-1, Grice or de Beaugrande as the organising axis.** They describe what readers achieve, or theories of conversation and text. None tells a check what input it needs, and ISO lacks faithfulness.
  - **What it taught:** cite them; do not organise by them.
- **A base reference made of examples only.** An example carries many features at once, so a finding cannot say which one was expected. That would break the base reference's own determinacy.
  - **What it taught:** keep rules, and attach examples to them as test pairs.
- **No written reference; write nothing until it fails.** For an AI drafter, silence is filled by its defaults, and the first failure is the costly one (14-41).
  - **What it taught:** growth from corrections is a route for low-stakes conventions, not a replacement for writing.
- **A purely advisory base reference** ("guidelines, not rules", applied to everything). A convention cannot make an ambiguous word pick out one thing.
  - **What it taught:** floors are met, for example by the workspace's terms, never lowered.
- **"The general guide always wins."** Google's order and PEP 8 say the opposite for conventions.
  - **What it taught:** that relation survives only for floors ("as a minimum").
- **The rule "change the surface only where it serves the whole".** Applied literally, it decides nothing at detection, because "serves the whole" is the text's purpose.
  - **What it taught:** a rule whose only test is purpose belongs to whoever holds the purpose.
- **A floors sentence in AlignCraft now.** Unexamined for code.
  - **What it taught:** the frontier is recorded as a DEFERRED item.
- **Renaming AlignCraft's "Two Types of Rules".** AlignCraft's own text, "Project Preferences: Specific to your tech stack, team, or domain", already covers domain and language rules. The claim that it contradicted itself was false.
  - **What it taught:** the base reference mirrors AlignCraft's two types as they are.
- **Rewriting earlier findings in place.** This project's overview: "Earlier findings retain their original proposals and qualifications, including where later work changed direction."
  - **What it taught:** correct by dated notes.

### Refined, and why

- **Test pairs for every rule** became test pairs for rules that claim to find problems, taken from corrections, with AI-written pairs confirmed by the owner. Conventions need no pair, and an AI-written pair would encode the AI's defaults.
- **Six fields per rule** became three per rule, plus three inherited per group. This answers AlignCraft's warning against "More explanation than benefit".
- **"Exceptions must be shown"** became "shown strictly", each alternative with its own result. This project's trial showed a loose showing leaks.
- **"The owner's preferences"** became "standing authorisations and refusals". As first written, it overlapped style and templates.
- **Floors for "high stakes"** became floors marked per item. Stakes set strictness; they are not a list of rules.
- **The workspace's terms as shared ground** gained an audience condition. The bank counts only "the setting plus the passage", so a client who lacks the glossary still meets a gap.
- **Conventions** must be written as decisions, never as claims about readers ("'Readers usually want…' is not a source").
- **"Standing"** is kept in AlignCraft and replaced in Communication Craft, where it collides.

### Survived, and why each held

- **Three principles by input.** A check cannot run without its input. The project's own peer taxonomy already groups the properties of a text this way.
- **Your three references in the first principle.** All three are judged on the text and its stated setting. Your names are kept for the parts.
- **The drafted rules.** Each comes from this project's own account of harmony, has a reason and exceptions, and reads only the text. They remain drafts.
- **One exception principle.** It explains every exemption the bank uses, and its strict form is the one this project's trial validated.
- **The purpose-holder decides "it would be worse".** Otherwise an AI's defaults would write a text and then excuse it.
- **Two adoption paths.** Decisions make no claim of truth; rules that claim to find problems cost recall if wrong.
- **The workspace part as a style sheet.** This is how book editing keeps general rules apart from one manuscript's decisions and departures.
- **Precedence written in.** An unstated precedence is itself an implicit contract.

### Iteration 1's lessons, as context

- **Iteration 1 answered the classification.** It was right that the base reference lives in the workspace, and that one reference serves writer and checker.
- **It filled the contents by mapping onto existing documents.** Several of those documents belonged to other projects.
- **This iteration's lesson:** the contents could be built from this project's own concepts. What was missing was the step from definition to rule, with reasons, exceptions and tests.

## Open Questions

### Monitoring

- **Do "something is off" corrections on AI drafts fall once the base reference is given upfront?** Observable in the writer-and-checker trial.
- **How fast does the "no written rule yet" record grow, and how many entries become rules?** Observable after ten checked texts.

### Blocked

- **The AlignCraft chapter:** waits on your decision (14-41).
- **The names:** "textual integrity" for the first principle, and the "standing" choice in Communication Craft. They wait on you.

### Research Frontiers

- **When a wording "demands" one particular thing:** the bank's open edge, and the three-gaps finding's "boundary of the implicit".
- **A text conveying something its author did not mean:** the conveyance finding's open question, which the "implication" property points at.
- **Floors for code.**

### Refinement Triggers

- **Reopen "three principles by input"** if a check is found whose input fits none of the three.
- **Reopen the exception principle** if strict showings on the new rules let wrong exceptions through, as the bank's first trial did.
- **Reopen "a workspace cannot lower a reading rule"** if a case shows a lowered reading rule that changes nothing for any reader the setting names.
- **Reopen "only explicitness is not shared with the AI"** when the drafting-side default check runs. Each result there either moves an expectation into "shared, dated" or confirms it must be written.
- **Reopen "the path of the telling" as its own rule** if, after the drafts are tested, no real text shows it failing apart from order or content. 07-57's watch.

- [Requirements quality characteristics, ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148 (arXiv 2408.10886)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2408.10886)
- [Editorial style sheets](https://clearsightbooks.com/using-editorial-style-sheets-to-save-time-improve-consistency/)
- [ESLint rule documentation (example rule page)](https://eslint.org/docs/latest/rules/multiline-comment-style)
- [Standards of textuality (de Beaugrande and Dressler; course resource)](https://cte.univ-setif2.dz/moodle/mod/resource/view.php?id=142261)

# Finding: implicit contracts in AI work: which parts to make explicit, and how AlignCraft should say it

## Changes from Prior

**Prior path:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_13-28__something-is-off-base-references-and-workspace-alignment/finding.md (called 13-28 below).
- 13-28 showed that "something is off" is a comparison against "what every text of its kind is expected to be".
- It said those expectations should be written in a base reference in the workspace.

**Revision trigger: your message.** You named "what every text of this kind should be" an **implicit contract**, and said it is missing from AlignCraft. You asked:
- in what format to add it, since AlignCraft "was born regarding … coding";
- which parts of AI work must be made explicit, since "there is definitely implicit parts of the AI work".

**What is kept from 13-28:**
- the two scopes;
- the base reference;
- one reference for writer and checker;
- the growth loop;
- "accountable, not automatic".

**What changes.** The AlignCraft proposal grows. 13-28 offered a short note in one of three places. This finding proposes one general chapter plus four small edits, because you confirmed the concept is missing and general.

**What is new:**
- the general concept, with its two states (implicit and explicit);
- the self-confirming loop;
- the boundary rule for "contract parts";
- AlignCraft's own "When to Guide vs When to Follow" as the code form of that boundary;
- a one-page draft chapter.

**Migration:**
- a dated note appended to 13-28;
- the memory note updated.

Both are done at conclusion.

**MUST/COULD drift:**
- **Prior text (13-28 MUST):** "Decide on AlignCraft: option (b), (a) or (c), or no change."
- **New text (MUST):** "Decide on the AlignCraft chapter 'Implicit Contracts' and its four edits, or one of the alternatives (widen 'Essential Ground Rules'; a Workcraft-only note)."
- **Rationale:** you confirmed the concept is missing from AlignCraft and is general. A single sentence would leave it implicit.

13-28's other MUSTs are carried unchanged: the marking session, the base reference index, and the writer-checker trial.

## Question

**Your message (verbatim in Source Input):**
> "What every text of this kind should be, this is implicit contract, and yes this is sth missing in aligncraft … which format it should be added there, because … align craft was born regarding … coding, and this is something more general … AI the way it's trained it has this implicit knowledge and it creates the text based on that judges the text … we don't like the results … we cannot also control it but if we make it explicit with references then it will be controllable and we can adjust over time … there is definitely implicit parts of the AI work … but we should make the contract parts explicit"

**The goal:**
- what an implicit contract is;
- why the AI's implicit contracts are the problem;
- which parts must be made explicit;
- how the contract stays explicit and adjustable;
- in what format AlignCraft should take this in;
- what follows for Communication Craft and for code.

## Finding Summary

**The answer, in one line**
- **Yes. Unwritten standing expectations are an implicit contract, and AlignCraft lacks the general concept.** Write the contract parts; leave competence implicit. AlignCraft can take this in as one general chapter, plus four small edits.

**Why the AI's implicit contract is the problem**
- **Where an expectation is unwritten, the AI does not leave it empty. It fills it with its trained defaults.** AlignCraft: "Without explicit guidance, AI defaults to what it 'thinks' is best."
- **The same defaults then judge the work,** so the AI can miss the very things it got wrong. You put it as "it creates the text based on that, judges the text based on that".
- **Training cannot be edited, read, or held to one workspace.** A written contract sits outside the model, so it can be stated, read and changed.

**The contract parts: what must be written**
- **Every standing expectation someone will hold the work to.** A standing expectation is what every output of its kind should be.
- **It may stay implicit on one of only three grounds:**
  - **shared:** everyone involved, the AI included, already does it the same way. This is contract law's "oh, of course" test. For the AI it must be checked, not assumed, and checked again after a model change;
  - **no difference:** any reasonable way of meeting it is fine for whoever will judge the result;
  - **declared elsewhere:** a standard you point to already says it.
- **High-stakes expectations are written regardless.**
- **Competence stays implicit:** how to write code, how to write a clear sentence. It could not all be written ("we can know more than we can tell"), and it does not need to be.

**How the contract stays explicit and adjustable**
- **Write the known contract parts.** Give them to the AI before it works, and check the work against the same contract.
- **The same correction twice → add a rule.** A rule that causes friction → refine it or remove it. This is AlignCraft's own "When you correct AI repeatedly → Add a rule".
- **Every rule carries its reason and its exceptions,** as AlignCraft's ground rules already do ("Why it matters", "Valid exceptions").
- **An AI may propose a rule. Only the owner adopts it.**

**How much control this gives**
- **Three controls that training cannot give:**
  - state what is expected;
  - audit it (read it, and challenge it);
  - adjust it over time.
- **It is partial.** The rules are still applied by the model's judgment.
- **Measured in this project:** with written rules, 79–82% of the gaps reported were real, against 65% for an unruled prompt.

**AlignCraft already holds the pieces**
- **Ground rules**, written for code.
- **"When to Guide vs When to Follow"**, which already draws this boundary for code:
  - guide (write it down) where the knowledge is yours;
  - follow (leave it to the AI) where "Best practices" or "Multiple valid approaches" will do.
- **"Implicit contracts that nothing documents"**, which appears only as a risk under coherence.
- **The Explicitness pillar:** "documented, not assumed".
- **What is missing is the one concept that ties them together.**

**The format proposed (your decision)**
- **One general chapter, "Implicit Contracts",** in Part II (Understanding AI Collaboration), placed just before "Essential Ground Rules".
- **"Essential Ground Rules" becomes its code instance.**
- **Four small edits:**
  - a bullet at Level 0 (Workspace);
  - a pointer at Coherence;
  - one sentence in "The Data Dump";
  - optionally, widening Part II's description beyond code.
- **No new pillar and no new layer.**
- **Alternatives:**
  - widen "Essential Ground Rules" to any output;
  - or state the concept only in Workcraft.

**For Communication Craft**
- **The base reference is the explicit contract for texts.**
- **This finding's boundary decides what goes into it.**

## Finding

### 1. Why this question

**13-28** showed that "noticing something is off" is a comparison against what every text of its kind is expected to be. It said those expectations should be written in a base reference that lives in the workspace.

**You then:**
- named those expectations an **implicit contract**;
- said AlignCraft lacks it;
- asked how to add a general idea to a methodology born for code;
- asked where the line falls between the parts of AI work that may stay implicit and the "contract parts" that must not.

### 2. What an implicit contract is

**Delegated work is held to two kinds of expectation:**
- **what this particular task needs:** the task description, or for messages, the substrate;
- **what every output of its kind should be:** what good code looks like in this project, what a status update must contain, how formal a message to a client is.

**The second kind is a standing contract.** It holds across tasks.

**It is implicit while unwritten, and explicit once written.** AlignCraft uses both words:
- "Ground rules are your contract with AI": explicit;
- "implicit contracts that nothing documents but everything depends on": implicit.

**This finding is about the standing contract.** The task's own expectations are already the task description's job.

### 3. Why the AI's implicit contract is the problem

**Unwritten expectations do not stay empty.**
- **The AI fills them with its trained defaults.** AlignCraft: "AI brings its own biases to every project … Without explicit guidance, AI defaults to what it 'thinks' is best."
- **The defaults then become the contract you actually get.** Software has a name for this, Hyrum's Law: whatever is not declared gets depended on anyway: "Users build against what the system actually does, not what its documentation says".

**The same defaults judge the work.** You put it as: "it creates the text based on that, judges the text based on that".
- An AI reviewing its own kind of work tends to judge by the defaults it wrote with.
- So it can miss the very things it got wrong.
- The project's own measure supports this: with written rules, 79–82% of reported gaps were real; with an unruled prompt, 65%.

**A colleague would learn the contract; the AI keeps forgetting it.**
- A new colleague learns the workspace's unwritten contract and keeps it.
- The AI starts each session with the same generic defaults. AlignCraft: "'Here are my ground rules…' <- Eventually falls out … AI: 'What ground rules?'".
- So a written contract is the AI's only lasting copy of what is expected.

**AlignCraft's Data Dump says half of this.** It says the AI "has no implicit understanding of your industry, company, or goals". That is true of *your* understanding. But the AI does bring implicit understanding of its own, and uses it wherever you are silent.

**Training cannot fix this.**
- You cannot edit it.
- You cannot read it.
- You cannot hold it to one workspace.

### 4. The contract parts: what must be written

**The rule.** Write every standing expectation someone will hold the work to, unless one of three grounds holds:

| Ground | What it means | Where it comes from |
|---|---|---|
| **Shared** | Everyone involved, the AI included, already does it the same way. | Contract law's test for implying a term: "so obvious that … they would testily suppress him with a common 'oh of course'". For the AI, "of course" comes from training, so check what it does without the rule. Check again after a model change, because defaults move. |
| **No difference** | Any reasonable way of meeting it is fine for whoever will judge the result. | The project's conveyance finding: "Any reasonable choice gives the same result". |
| **Declared elsewhere** | A standard you point to already says it. | e.g. a style guide or a published standard |

**Two qualifications:**
- **Write high-stakes expectations regardless.** The first failure would cost too much to wait for a correction.
- **How detailed the contract must be follows the stakes.** The conveyance finding: "A contract or a prompt to an AI agent needs fine grain".

**Competence stays implicit:** how to write code, how to reason, how to write a clear sentence.
- Polanyi: "we can know more than we can tell".
- AlignCraft warns that over-written rules cause "Overly restrictive development".

**AlignCraft already draws this line for code,** in "When to Guide vs When to Follow":
- **"Guide When":** "Domain-specific knowledge required … Business logic involved … External constraints exist … Past decisions affect current … User experience matters". These are expectations that are yours: write them.
- **"Follow When":** "Multiple valid approaches exist" (no difference); "Best practices needed … Common patterns apply" (shared). Leave these to the AI's defaults.

The match is one of purpose, not of shared words. Both chapters ask who should supply the expectation.

**This is the conveyance finding's structure, one level up.**
- A text may leave something unsaid only if it is supplied, makes no difference, or is declared open.
- A workspace may leave an expectation unwritten only if it is shared, makes no difference, or is declared elsewhere.

### 5. How the contract stays explicit and adjustable

1. **Write the known contract parts first,** including every high-stakes expectation.
2. **Give them to the AI before it works,** as AlignCraft's ground rules are given: "establishing clear expectations upfront".
3. **Check the work against the same written contract.**
4. **When the same correction is made twice, an implicit part has failed. Add a rule.** AlignCraft: "When you correct AI repeatedly → Add a rule".
5. **When a rule causes friction, refine it or remove it.** AlignCraft: "When rules cause friction → Refine or remove".
6. **Give every rule its reason and its exceptions.** AlignCraft's ground rules already do this: "Why it matters … Valid exceptions". A rule with its reason can be applied with judgment, and changed when the reason no longer holds.
7. **An AI may propose a rule; only the owner adopts it.** This keeps the project's rule that an AI never settles its own standards.

### 6. How much control writing gives

**Writing gives three things training cannot:**
- **State:** what is expected.
- **Audit:** read it and challenge it. Constitutional AI, a known method for steering AI with written principles, rests on this: "The only human oversight is provided through a list of rules or principles".
- **Adjust:** change a rule and re-test it.

**The control is partial.** The rules are still interpreted by the model's own judgment. 13-28 called this "accountable, not automatic":
- every judgment can be traced to something you can read and change;
- but it is not mechanical.

### 7. AlignCraft: what is there, what is missing, the format

**What AlignCraft already has:**
- **its scope:** "applicable to AI-Human and AI-AI collaboration, with a focus on software development";
- **Part I's core concepts** (the alignment chain, artifacts, pillars), which are general;
- **Part II, "Understanding AI Collaboration",** which explains how AI behaves: attention, context, drift, ground rules, and guide against follow;
- **Part III's patterns,** which are about code.

**What it lacks.** The general concept that ties together four things it already has:
- ground rules;
- Guide/Follow;
- "implicit contracts";
- the Explicitness pillar.

**The proposed format:** one general chapter in Part II, placed just before "Essential Ground Rules", with four small edits. The draft, with the critique's fixes applied:

> **Implicit Contracts**
>
> Delegated work is held to two kinds of expectation: what this task needs — that is the task description — and what every output of its kind should be: what good code looks like in this project, what a status update must contain, how formal a message to a client is. The second kind is a standing contract.
>
> Most of that contract is never written down. Between people that often works: a new colleague learns the unwritten parts and keeps them. AI does not. Where you don't state an expectation, the AI fills it with its trained defaults — what it "thinks" is best — and those defaults become the contract you get. And when you ask the same AI to review the work, it tends to judge by the same defaults, so it can miss the very things it got wrong.
>
> You cannot change the training. You can change what the AI reads. A written contract sits outside the model: you can state it, read it, and change it.
>
> **Which parts to write.** Write every standing expectation someone will hold the work to, unless one of these holds:
> - *Shared* — everyone involved, the AI included, already does it the same way. Ask: if someone proposed writing it down, would everyone say "of course"? For the AI, don't assume: check what it does without the rule, and check again when the model changes.
> - *No difference* — any reasonable way of meeting it is fine for whoever will judge the result.
> - *Declared elsewhere* — a standard you point to already says it.
>
> Write high-stakes expectations regardless. Competence — how to write code, how to write a clear sentence — stays implicit: you could not write it all down, and you don't need to. This is the line *When to Guide vs When to Follow* draws: follow where common practice or several valid approaches will do; guide where the knowledge, constraints or preferences are yours.
>
> **How the contract grows.** Write the known contract parts and give them to the AI before it works. Check the work against the same written contract. When you correct the AI for the same thing twice, an implicit part has failed: add a rule. When a rule causes friction, refine or remove it. Give each rule its reason and its exceptions, so it can be applied — and changed — with judgment. The AI may propose rules; you adopt them.
>
> A written contract does not make the AI's work automatic: the rules are still applied by the model's judgment. It makes every judgment traceable to something you can read and change.
>
> **Instances.** Code: *Essential Ground Rules* (next chapter). Alignment documents: *What Makes an Artifact Aligned*. Messages and other texts: a base reference of what every text of its kind should be.
>
> This is the Explicitness pillar — *documented, not assumed* — applied to the standing expectations of your work.

**The four small edits:**

| Where | Proposed text |
|---|---|
| **Level 0, Workspace**, in "This means understanding:" | "- What every output is expected to meet — the standing contract — written down (see *Implicit Contracts*)" |
| **Coherence**, after "implicit contracts that nothing documents but everything depends on" | "*Implicit Contracts* explains how to find them and write them down." |
| **The Data Dump**, after "has no implicit understanding of your industry, company, or goals" | "It does bring defaults of its own, and it will use them wherever you are silent." |
| **Part II's description** (optional) | "How AI thinks about code and other work, and effective collaboration" |

**Why this format.**
- **Part II is where AlignCraft explains how AI behaves.** Filling silence with defaults is AI behaviour.
- **"Essential Ground Rules" keeps all its content,** framed as the code instance.
- **The Level 0 bullet makes the concept visible in the core chain.** Without it, a reader of Part I would meet "implicit contracts" only as a risk under coherence.

**Alternatives, if you prefer:**
- **Widen "Essential Ground Rules" into ground rules for any output.** This is smaller, but it buries the concept inside a chapter about code.
- **State the concept only in Workcraft's definition of Alignment.** This leaves AlignCraft as it is, although it lacks the concept.
- **A larger option, deferred:** rescope AlignCraft to any delegated AI work, with code as one domain.

**What not to do:**
- **Not a new pillar.** The pillar chain is fixed, and this is the Explicitness pillar applied, linked by purpose. No pillar is a tool.
- **Not a seventh layer.** 13-28 showed that standing expectations run across all six layers.

### 8. For Communication Craft and for code

| Domain | Its explicit contract | Where it lives |
|---|---|---|
| Code | ground rules (universal rules, project preferences) | GROUND_RULES.md |
| Alignment documents | "What Makes an Artifact Aligned" | AlignCraft |
| Messages and other texts | the base reference | the workspace (13-28's index) |

**The texts' base reference holds:**
- the explicitness bank;
- the non-ambiguity patterns N1–N6;
- R07–R09;
- the genre and relationship catalogues;
- house preferences;
- the rules still to write.

**This finding's boundary decides what goes in.** Its mechanism decides how it grows.

### 9. Outside grounding

| Source | What it says | What it supports |
|---|---|---|
| **Contract law** | A term is implied only if it is "necessary to give business efficacy to the contract" (The Moorcock, 1889), or "so obvious that it goes without saying" (the officious bystander, Shirlaw v Southern Foundries). Everything else is an express term. | the "shared" ground |
| **Hyrum's Law** | "with a sufficient number of users of an API, it does not matter what the contract promises because all observable behaviors will be depended on by somebody" | undeclared defaults become the de facto contract |
| **Constitutional AI** (Bai et al., 2022) | "The only human oversight is provided through a list of rules or principles" | written principles steer and judge AI output |
| **Polanyi** | "we can know more than we can tell" | competence stays implicit |

## Inherited Commitments Re-test

- **Commitment:** two scopes of what was meant (standing, particular).
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** the standing scope is the contract, and the particular scope is the task description (section 2).

- **Commitment:** the base reference in the workspace; one reference for writer and checker.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** it is the explicit contract for texts, given upfront and checked against (sections 5 and 8).

- **Commitment:** the growth loop; "accountable, not automatic".
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** the loop now carries four additions (section 5):
    - rules with reasons and exceptions;
    - owner adoption;
    - re-checking defaults after a model change;
    - writing high-stakes expectations regardless.

- **Commitment:** an AlignCraft note in one of three places, or no change.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment found INVALID as the main proposal.
  - **Evidence:** you confirmed the concept is general and missing. A single note would leave it implicit. Replaced by a chapter plus four edits; the note options survive inside the edits (section 7).

**From 09-00** (devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_09-00__harmony-as-alignment-aligncraft-layers-in-communication/finding.md):

- **Commitment:** the pillars are how context is uncovered so it can be aligned.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** the chapter applies the Explicitness pillar's own words to standing expectations. It is linked by purpose, and no tool is mapped onto a pillar.

**From the conveyance finding** (devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-09_16-52__conveyance-what-a-text-may-leave-unsaid/finding.md):

- **Commitment:** a text may leave something unsaid only if it is supplied, makes no difference, or is declared open.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** the same structure decides what a workspace may leave unwritten (section 4).

## Next Actions

### MUST

- **What:** Append a dated note to 13-28, and update the memory note on communication alignment.
  - **Who:** this session.
  - **Gate:** now. Done.
  - **Why:** 13-28's AlignCraft proposal is superseded.

- **What:** Decide on the AlignCraft chapter "Implicit Contracts" and its four edits, or on an alternative.
  - **Who:** you, as AlignCraft's owner.
  - **Gate:** your call.
  - **Why:** the concept is missing from AlignCraft, and you asked for the format.

- **What:** Test the boundary on 10 expectations taken from real corrections.
  - **For each:** apply the three grounds, and run the "of course" check on your side and on the AI's default behaviour without the rule.
  - **Who:** a test inquiry.
  - **Gate:** before the chapter is published.
  - **Why:** the boundary has not yet been run on real cases.

- **What (carried from 13-28):**
  - the marking session;
  - the base reference index;
  - the writer-and-checker trial.

### COULD

- **What:** Test the self-confirming loop. The same AI writes and then reviews, with and without the base reference; count the defaults the reviewer accepts.
  - **Who:** a test inquiry.
  - **Gate:** with the writer-and-checker trial.
  - **Why:** evidence for the loop you described.

- **What:** Give every base-reference rule "Why it matters" and "Valid exceptions".
  - **Who:** the base reference index's author.
  - **Gate:** with the index.
  - **Why:** rules can then be applied and changed with judgment.

- **What:** Re-run the default check after each model change, on the expectations left implicit as "shared".
  - **Who:** the maintainer of the detection and fix skills.
  - **Gate:** the next model change.
  - **Why:** defaults drift.

### DEFERRED

- **What:** Rescope AlignCraft to any delegated AI work.
  - **Gate:** when AlignCraft gains a second domain beyond code, or you ask.
  - **Why (if revived):** the concept would then sit in a general book.

## Reasoning

**Killed:**
- **"The AI has no implicit contract."** AlignCraft itself contradicts it: "AI brings its own biases"; "Follow When: Best practices needed". What it left behind: the Data Dump sentence.
- **Writing competence down.** Polanyi's point, and AlignCraft's warning against overly restrictive rules.
- **A new pillar or layer.** Already killed in 13-28.

**Refined:**
- **"Corrections alone build the contract."** Refined to: write the known and high-stakes parts upfront; grow the rest from corrections.
- **"Shared with the AI."** Refined to: provisional, re-checked after model changes.
- **The scope of "implicit contract".** Refined to: standing expectations only.
- **The draft's "rarely finds them wrong".** Refined to: "can miss the very things it got wrong".

**Survived:**
- **The three grounds.** They fit contract law's test, the conveyance finding, and AlignCraft's own Guide/Follow.
- **The mechanism.** It is AlignCraft's own evolution strategy, with reasons and exceptions as its ground rules already have.
- **The chapter format.** Part II explains how AI behaves, and AlignCraft already holds every ingredient.

## Open Questions

### Monitoring

- **Do corrections on AI work fall once the contract is written and given upfront?** Observable in the writer-and-checker trial.

### Blocked

- **The AlignCraft text** waits on your decision.

### Research Frontiers

- **How to check "shared with the AI" cheaply, at scale.**
- **Contracts for AI-to-AI handoffs.**

### Refinement Triggers

- **Reopen the boundary** if, in the 10-expectation test, an expectation that met a ground still caused corrections.
- **Reopen the format** if readers of the chapter cannot tell what to write down for their own domain.

- **Section 8's list of what the texts' base reference holds is replaced.**
  - It named other local projects' catalogues and preference lists.
  - The user ruled this project standalone: "dont refer to local different prjects or their skills".
- **What the base reference holds instead,** built from this project alone:
  - three principles: textual integrity; faithful; fit;
  - the rules under them: the explicitness bank as the tested part, and drafted rules for consistency, complementing and readable form, each with its reason and exceptions;
  - parts for each kind of text;
  - the workspace's own decisions, kept as a style sheet;
  - cited standards.
- **Unchanged:**
  - the boundary in section 4;
  - the AlignCraft chapter and its four edits in section 7. The later finding adds nothing to the chapter.
- **"Shared with the AI" must be checked before it is relied on.** Only explicitness is measured.
