# Finding: what to do next to actually create Communication Craft: the substrate, inside one small working loop

## Changes from Prior

**Prior paths:**
- **The development-order finding:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_12-40__communication-substrate-development-order/finding.md (called 12-40 below). It answered an earlier version of this question: develop the substrate through "one useful communication operation, together with the specific understanding needed to perform and inspect it".
- **The base-reference finding:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_13-28__something-is-off-base-references-and-workspace-alignment/finding.md (called 13-28 below). It set out what the written rules for texts hold, and listed MUSTs to write and test them.

**Revision trigger: your new question, and your standalone ruling.**
- 12-40's concrete starting case, and the evidence behind it, came from another project's exchanges and guidance.
- Under your rule ("this is a standalone seperate project … dont refer to local different prjects or their skills"), that case is gone. So 12-40's general answer needed a new, in-bounds case.

**What is preserved:**
- **From 12-40:**
  - develop the substrate through one bounded use that runs the whole path;
  - no complete general substrate first;
  - share parts only when two uses show they are common.
- **From 13-28:** the base reference's structure, and its drafted rules as things to test.

**What has changed:**
- **The first use:** another project's operational updates are replaced by the AI's own reports and findings to you.
- **13-28's MUST to write the full base reference now is narrowed.** Only its workspace part (the rules for AI-written texts to you) is written now; the full document waits until a second use.

**What is new:**
- the plan's order: a baseline from your past corrections; a written agreement; one added substrate step; a hand-run loop; a blinded comparison; checkpoints;
- an answer-extraction test;
- the recognition that this project's memory notes and finding template already hold part of the needed rules;
- a recommended minimum fix for the public README.

**MUST/COULD drift:**
- **Against 13-28.**
  - **Prior MUST:** "Write the first version of the base reference document: the three principles; the explicitness bank linked as the tested part; the drafted rules marked 'draft'; kind parts for the substrate and alignment documents; an empty six-slot workspace style sheet; a cited-standards list; a short 'how it works' …".
  - **New:** "Write the reply agreement (the base reference's workspace part for AI-written texts to the owner) now; write the full document after the second use."
  - **Rationale:** rules written for every text from one use would overfit to one reader.
- **Against 12-40.**
  - **Prior COULD:** "Develop and test the operational question bank with materialization checks and content questions distinguished, using the four wording-only revisions as initial failure evidence."
  - **New:** the first loop on the AI's texts to you, with your past corrections as the first evidence.
  - **Rationale:** the earlier evidence came from another project.

**Migration:** dated notes are appended to 12-40 and 13-28 at conclusion.

## Question

**Your words:**
> "think about what are the next things we should do if [we] want to actually create this … communication craft — should we focus on substrate or some other things"

**The goal:**
- a recommendation on what to do next;
- whether the substrate should be the focus;
- an ordered set of next steps;
- the reasons and the criterion behind them.

The question is kept open to six readings:
- a focus decision;
- an ordered roadmap;
- the smallest working version;
- the main blockers;
- theory or implementation next;
- a criterion for choosing.

## Finding Summary

**The answer, in one line**
- **Yes, the substrate is the focus: it is the main thing to build next. But build it inside one small, complete loop where it can be tested, not on its own.**

**What "the substrate" means here**
- **The substrate** is the written brief of what a text must achieve and say, and in what order, made before the text is written. In your design diagram it sits between the raw context and the text.
- **The first version is small but keeps the shape:**
  - your literal questions, ranked;
  - a one-line answer to each;
  - pointers to what supports each answer;
  - terms that must be defined;
  - the order.

**Why the substrate is the focus**
- **Everything Communication Craft still lacks depends on it:**
  - the check that the brief captures what is needed;
  - the check that the text follows the brief;
  - creating texts at all.
- **The only parts built and measured so far** are the explicitness detector and fixer. They check a text on its own, with no brief.

**Why not on its own**
- **A brief can only be judged by the text it leads to and by the reader's reaction.** A text can follow an incomplete brief perfectly ("A draft can faithfully express an incomplete essence").
- **Your own methodology and outside practice agree:**
  - AlignCraft: "Start fuzzy. Build. Learn. Solidify.";
  - Gall's Law: "A complex system that works is invariably found to have evolved from a simple system that worked".

**Why now**
- **Since 7 October about twenty findings have been written.** The only implementation work in that time was revising the explicitness bank.
- **The overview still says** "The full proposed loop has not yet been established as a tested implementation".

**The first use: the AI's reports and findings to you**
- **It is the only real communication stream in this project** that has a present reader, real corrections, and no other project's material.
- **Your corrections are its first evidence:**
  - "you didn't actually answer my question";
  - "define terms first";
  - "explain better what happened and what was the verdict".
- **Short replies test whether your question is answered first.** Findings also test choosing what matters from a lot of material.
- **A second, different use comes after:** a message to someone else, built from sources.

**The first steps, in order**
1. **Baseline:**
   - read the texts you corrected, filtered to this project only, with your consent;
   - run fresh checks on them;
   - write the success thresholds before anything runs.
2. **Agreement:** you adopt a short written agreement for AI texts to you, drawn from the corrections already in my memory notes.
3. **One added step:** the substrate step before each reply and finding, in a file the AI reads at every session start.
4. **Hand-run** for about 20 texts, checks batched per session, your quick mark on each.
5. **Blinded comparison:** the same cases written without the step, shown to you unlabelled and in random order.
6. **Checkpoint:**
   - keep, simplify or extend the step;
   - package what survived as Communication Craft's first creation skill.
7. **Second use;** then the full written rules, shared parts, and publishing.

**How it is judged**
- **An answer-extraction test.** A fresh AI is given only your question and the text. It must quote the sentence that answers it, say where it is, and say whether it is direct.
- **Your marks.**
- **The blinded comparison.**

**The other things, and when**

| Other thing | When |
|---|---|
| the explicitness checks | reused now |
| the base reference | its part for your texts now; the rest after the second use |
| theory consolidation | one page now; fully later |
| more checks, receiving, integration | later, as uses demand |
| publishing | a minimum now, your call; fully later |

**Your decisions**
- **The public README** still says "Communication — v1 complete" and lists skills you have ruled outside this project. A one-line pointer now is the recommended minimum.
- Whether you prefer a different first use.
- Where the procedure file is pointed from.
- Consent to use your session transcripts.

## Finding

### 1. Why this question

**Communication Craft is the communication part of Workcraft,** your framework for automating work through AI. Its overview names two parts that should "improve each other":
- **theory and methodology;**
- **implementable skills** that AI can execute.

Its design diagram runs:
- **Raw Context** (everything the exchange starts from);
- **the Comm Substrate** (the written brief);
- **Materialisation** (the text);
- with an alignment check over each pair.

**You asked what to do next to "actually create" it, and whether to focus on the substrate.**
- An earlier inquiry, 12-40, asked nearly the same question two days ago.
- Its concrete case was another project's material, which your standalone ruling now excludes.
- So the question needed a new, in-bounds answer.

### 2. The direct answer, and three different "firsts"

**Yes, the substrate is the focus. It is the main thing to build next, but inside one small, complete loop, not on its own.**

12-40 pointed out that "first" means three different things, and your question touches all three:

| Kind of "first" | Answer |
|---|---|
| **Within one text being written** | The substrate comes first: decide what to say, then say it. |
| **In learning** | What teaches the most comes first: a complete loop, because only the text and the reader's reaction show whether the brief was right. |
| **In building** | The substrate comes first among the missing parts. It is built small, together with what is needed to test it. |

### 3. Where Communication Craft stands

**Built and measured:**
- **the explicitness detector:** 79–82% of the gaps it reports are real, against 65% for an AI with no written rules;
- **the explicitness fixer:** with author answers, 50 of 56 planted gaps resolved;
- **two benchmarks;**
- **a written method for building any new check** (the "codebook detector" method), with rules for measuring honestly.

**Theory only:** the substrate, with these parts written in findings and no implementation:
- its 14-facet map;
- the "need";
- meaning units;
- how answers are formed;
- the shape that ranks requirements and the points that answer them.

Also theory only: the two alignment checks, and the base reference (whose rules are drafted, not tested).

**Not existing:**
- any end-to-end loop;
- any substrate form or tool;
- any way to measure whether a created text is good.

**The blockers, ranked:**
1. No end-to-end loop.
2. No in-bounds test case for the substrate. Its earlier trials used another project's exchanges.
3. No measure of creation quality.
4. Theory scattered across about twenty unconsolidated findings.
5. A public face that is wrong (section 12).

### 4. Why the substrate, and why not alone

**The substrate is the keystone.**
- Both alignment checks compare against it.
- Creating a text starts from it.
- Without it, Communication Craft can only check finished texts on their own, which is what the explicitness skills already do.

**A substrate cannot be tested on its own.**
- 19-33 (the finding on the creation loop) found that "A draft can faithfully express an incomplete essence".
- Agreement between brief and text proves nothing about whether the brief was right. Only the reader's reaction does.

**Your own methodology says the same.** AlignCraft's method for starting a new project with AI:
- simplifies the concepts for a first version ("The simplification should reduce features, not break architecture");
- then builds module by module, with real tests ("Validate Real Success, Not Just 'No Exception'").

Its "Fuzzy Architecture" chapter ends: "Start fuzzy. Build. Learn. Solidify."

**Outside practice agrees:**
- **Gall's Law:** "A complex system designed from scratch never works and cannot be patched up to make it work."
- **The "walking skeleton":** "a tiny implementation of the system that performs a small end-to-end function … it should link together the main architectural components".
- **Anthropic's advice for building with AI models:** "finding the simplest solution possible, and only increasing complexity when needed".

### 5. The first use: the AI's reports and findings to you

**Why this use.**
- **It is the only communication stream in this project with all four:**
  - a real reader who is present (you);
  - real failures, with your corrections;
  - material at hand;
  - nothing from another project.
- **Your outgoing messages to other people would likely concern other projects.** Synthetic or public texts have no reader to judge them.

**Its two kinds of text:**
- **Short replies.** Most of your corrections were on these, and they have no written template yet. They test whether your literal question gets answered first.
- **Findings.** They already have a template and style rules (the conclude protocol of Traverse). They test choosing what matters from a lot of material: six step outputs per inquiry.

**What it cannot test.** The substrate's hardest parts:
- choosing the needed meaning from sources;
- the different ways an answer is formed: already decided by the author, gathered from sources, produced by new reasoning, asked of the author, or left open.

A second, different use must test those: a message to someone else built from sources. For example, an update about this project's own work for an outside reader the setting names.

**Alternatives for the first use** (your decision):
- **messages between AI workers and a coordinating AI.** Workcraft's README names this as a Communication use;
- **your own outgoing messages,** if they concern this project.

**How your corrections count.**
- **They are outcomes** for texts addressed to you: did the text work for its reader?
- **They are your workspace's conventions.**
- **They are not rules for every text** until a second use supports them.

This keeps the project's rule against basing checks on reader profiles. Your judgments measure results; they do not decide what a text means.

### 6. Part of what is needed already exists, in other forms

- **My memory notes already act as a first set of rules for texts to you.** Each records your correction in your words, why it matters, and how to apply it:
  - "define terms first";
  - "answer the literal question first";
  - "generic, unmixed concepts".

  That is the base reference's rule format. It needs filtering (section 7) and your adoption.
- **The conclude protocol is already a written standard for findings:** a template, a non-ambiguity principle, plain-language style rules and a quality test.
- **The explicitness detector** can check texts to you, with a setting sentence that says they are for you. Its reliability on this kind of text is not yet known.
- **The project's codebook method** tells how to build any further check, and how to measure it honestly.

### 7. The first loop, step by step

**1. Baseline.**
- Find the AI texts behind your past corrections in the session transcripts.
- Sort each failure:
  - communication: selection, order, terms, plainness, faithfulness;
  - or quality of the work itself. "You're running traverses so fast" belongs to Workcraft's Semantic Formulation, not here.
- Run the answer-extraction test and the explicitness detector on those texts, in fresh AI sessions.
- **Write the success thresholds and the checkpoint rules before anything runs.**

**2. Clean the data first.**
- **The memory notes mix communication rules with process rules,** and at least one names another project's files.
- **This session's own transcript contains another project's material:** an earlier explicitness run on another project's report.
- So:
  - keep only communication rules;
  - remove other projects' names;
  - use only exchanges about this project;
  - ask your consent first.

**3. The agreement.** You adopt the filtered rules as a short written agreement for AI texts to you. This is the base reference's workspace part for this use.

**4. One added step,** in a short file the AI reads at the start of every session (you choose where it is pointed from). Before each reply and finding:
- list your literal questions, ranked;
- write a one-line answer to each;
- point to what supports each answer;
- mark the terms that must be defined;
- then write, opening with the answers.

This is the substrate's first version. "Bottom line up front", the US Army's writing standard, is the same idea: "Put the recommendation, conclusion or reason for writing -- the bottom line -- in the first or second paragraph, not at the end."

**5. Run it by hand for about 20 texts, replies and findings both.**
- Fresh AI checks run in a batch at the end of each session, not before each reply, so replies stay fast.
- You give each text a quick first-read mark: does the opening answer your question?

**6. The blinded comparison.**
- Some held-out cases are also written without the added step.
- You see the two versions unlabelled and in random order, and pick which works better.

**7. Checkpoint, using the rules written in step 1:**
- **keep** the step if it opens with direct answers more often and your marks agree;
- **simplify it to the agreement alone** if the two versions tie;
- **extend it** only for a failure that recurs and a field would catch.

Then package what survived as Communication Craft's first creation skill: a skill for reports to you.

**This is "write the agreement and add one step before building any new tool".** New tools are built only for what the agreement and the step cannot fix. They are never built just to fill a plan.

### 8. How it is judged

**The answer-extraction test.** A fresh AI gets only your literal question and the text. It must:
- quote the sentence that answers the question;
- say where it is: the first paragraph, or later;
- say whether the answer is direct, or only implied.

This measures "did it answer my question" without your time. It uses only the question and the text, so it is a property of the text, not a profile of a reader. It is recorded as a check of your workspace's convention, not as a rule for every text.

**Your marks:** the quick first-read mark on each text, plus your choice in the blinded comparison.

**Checkable details:**
- the direct answer is first;
- undefined terms, counted by the explicitness detector;
- every claim traceable to the finding;
- every part of the text has a place in the brief.

**A second automatic check:** does the drafting AI follow the brief, carrying every point and in order? Here the substrate is itself a communication, read by the AI that writes the text.

**What is not yet known:**
- whether the answer-extraction test agrees with your marks;
- whether the loop reduces your corrections.

The run will show both.

### 9. The other things, and when

| Other thing | When | Why |
|---|---|---|
| The explicitness checks | reused now | already built and measured |
| The base reference (13-28) | the workspace part for your texts now (the agreement); its drafted rules tested on the loop's texts; the full document after the second use | one use cannot show which rules hold for every text |
| Theory consolidation | one page now, a current model of the substrate checked against the findings; full consolidation after the loop | building on scattered, revised concepts would build the wrong thing, but solidifying before testing is the opposite mistake |
| More checks (whole-text consistency, faithfulness to sources) | later, by the codebook method, as uses demand | their need will show in the outcome record |
| Receiving incoming texts | later | — |
| Integration with Workcraft's other parts | later | gathering material belongs to Context Accumulation; producing answers belongs to Semantic Formulation |
| Publishing | a minimum now (section 12); fully after the second use | — |

### 10. The criterion for choosing what comes next

**Build what closes the loop on a real use, with a reader available to judge, at the least new effort.** In short: evidence per unit of effort.

The same criterion decides at each checkpoint.

### 11. The order after the first loop

- **After the first checkpoint:** a second, different use that exercises choosing from sources and the different ways an answer is formed.
- **After the second use:**
  - write the full base reference;
  - extract what both uses share (a substrate skill; rules for every text), using 12-40's tests for sharing;
  - then consolidate the theory and publish.

### 12. The public README

**The repository's README says "Communication — v1 complete".**
- It lists the older workflows and skills that you have since ruled outside this project.
- Its links point outside the repository.
- The Communication Craft overview links to findings that are not in the repository.

**Options (your decision, since it is public):**
- **Recommended minimum:** a one-line pointer now ("Communication Craft is being rebuilt; see communicationcraft/"), and a full description after the loop.
- **A full rewrite now.**
- **Leave it.** This keeps a false public claim.

### 13. Outside grounding

| Source | What it says | What it supports |
|---|---|---|
| Gall's Law | "A complex system that works is invariably found to have evolved from a simple system that worked." | build a small whole first |
| The walking skeleton (Cockburn) | "a tiny implementation of the system that performs a small end-to-end function … it should link together the main architectural components" | one complete loop |
| Anthropic, "Building effective agents" | "finding the simplest solution possible, and only increasing complexity when needed" | agreement before tools |
| Evals practice (Husain) | looking at your data is "90% of the whole eval process"; discover what to measure through error analysis | the baseline from your corrections |
| US Army writing standard (BLUF) | "Put the recommendation, conclusion or reason for writing -- the bottom line -- in the first or second paragraph, not at the end." | the answer-first step and its test |
| Fowler, the harvested platform (via 12-40) | shared platforms are harvested from working applications | share parts after two uses |

## Inherited Commitments Re-test

- **Commitment:** "Establish the substrate first as a usable, revisable basis".
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_17-43__communication-craft-foundation-for-workcraft/finding.md (Finding Summary).
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** "first" holds within one text. As a build order, the substrate is first among the missing parts, built inside a loop (section 2).

- **Commitment:** the creation loop (form the meaning → express it → review → diagnose → revise); "Agreement between those two is therefore insufficient".
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_19-33__communication-substrate-materialization-and-review-loop/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** it is the reason the substrate cannot be tested alone (section 4).

- **Commitment:** the substrate is a working understanding; the 14-facet map.
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_22-09__communication-substrate-foundation-and-dimensions/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** the first version uses a minimal subset that keeps the architecture (need → points → support → order). The full map is a later sweep, not the first form.

- **Commitment:** develop through one bounded use that runs the whole path; no complete general substrate first; share only what two uses show is common.
  - **Source:** 12-40.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** the general answer holds. Its case (another project's updates) is replaced by the AI's texts to you (section 5).

- **Commitment:** how an answer is formed (decided, gathered, produced, asked, or left open).
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-09_17-15__essence-formation-moves-revisited/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** INHERITED-WITHOUT-RE-TEST.
  - **Reason:** the first use cannot exercise them; the second use must (section 5).

- **Commitment:** the substrate needs a shape: "which requirement matters most, which point answers it".
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-09_19-31__the-substrate-concept-that-arranges-the-message/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** it is the core of the first substrate step (ranked questions, answers first). Its own test case was another project's, so the first loop is its first in-bounds test.

- **Commitment:** the design diagram is correct; each check = a check of the document itself plus comparisons; detect, then decide against settled meaning.
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_11-29__alignment-checks-and-textual-integrity-in-the-commcraft-design/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** the first loop has both checks: brief against raw context, and text against brief plus the text's own integrity.

- **Commitment:** what the base reference holds; its MUSTs.
  - **Source:** 13-28.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** its workspace part is written now, as the agreement. Its drafted rules are tested in the loop. The full document is narrowed to after the second use (drift recorded above).

- **Commitment:** write unless shared, makes no difference, or is already in a cited standard; an AI proposes, the owner adopts.
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_14-41__implicit-contracts-explicit-references-and-aligncraft/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** the agreement turns your corrections into written rules that you adopt (section 7).

## Next Actions

### MUST

- **What:** Decide the README minimum: a one-line pointer now, a full rewrite now, or leave it.
  - **Who:** you (it is public).
  - **Gate:** any time; recommended before the loop starts.
  - **Why:** the README states "Communication — v1 complete" for skills outside this project.

- **What:** Give consent to use your session transcripts, filtered to this project, as test material, and choose where the procedure file is pointed from (memory or a project instruction file).
  - **Who:** you.
  - **Gate:** before the baseline.
  - **Why:** the baseline needs your past corrected texts, and the step must be read in every session.

- **What:** Build the baseline:
  - the filtered agreement drawn from the memory notes, for your adoption;
  - the corrected texts and the failure sorting;
  - fresh-agent checks on them;
  - success thresholds and checkpoint rules, written before any run.
  - **Who:** this session or the next, with you adopting the agreement.
  - **Gate:** after your consent.
  - **Why:** the substrate's fields must come from real failures, and the result must be able to change a decision.

- **What:** Write the one-page current model of the substrate, checked against the substrate findings and the contradictions noted in the 8 October review.
  - **Who:** the next session.
  - **Gate:** before the added step is written.
  - **Why:** the step must use the current, consistent definition.

- **What:** Run the first loop:
  - the added step, in a file read at every session start;
  - about 20 replies and findings;
  - batched fresh checks;
  - your first-read marks;
  - the blinded comparison;
  - then the checkpoint.
  - **Who:** the AI in your sessions; you mark.
  - **Gate:** after the baseline and the one-page model.
  - **Why:** it is the first test of the substrate and of the whole loop.

### COULD

- **What:** Package what survives the checkpoint as Communication Craft's first creation skill (reports to the owner).
  - **Who:** the next build session.
  - **Gate:** after the checkpoint.
  - **Why:** builds only what the evidence supports.
  - **Depends-on:** MUST item "Run the first loop". This COULD is GATED — do not act until the MUST resolves.

- **What:** Choose and run the second use: a message to someone else built from sources.
  - **Who:** a later inquiry with you.
  - **Gate:** after the checkpoint.
  - **Why:** tests the substrate's hard parts (choosing from sources; how answers are formed).
  - **Depends-on:** MUST item "Run the first loop". This COULD is GATED — do not act until the MUST resolves.

- **What:** Record the explicitness detector's reliability on texts written for you.
  - **Who:** within the loop's batched checks.
  - **Gate:** during the first loop.
  - **Why:** it was measured only on other kinds of text.
  - **Depends-on:** MUST item "Run the first loop". OVERRIDE: adoption-ready despite the open MUST. Reason: the measurement can start on the baseline texts before the loop runs.

- **What:** Relabel benchmark 3's answer key without the other catalogue's pattern names, and check where benchmark 2's excerpts come from.
  - **Who:** the benchmark maintainer.
  - **Gate:** the next benchmark edit.
  - **Why:** keeps the published benchmarks standalone.

### DEFERRED

- **What:** Write the full base reference (rules for every text, kind parts, cited standards); extract shared parts; consolidate and publish the theory.
  - **Gate:** after the second use.
  - **Why (if revived):** rules shown to hold in two different uses can be stated for every text.

- **What:** More checks by the codebook method (whole-text consistency; faithfulness to sources); receiving; integration with Context Accumulation and Semantic Formulation.
  - **Gate:** when the outcome record shows a recurring failure one of them would catch.
  - **Why (if revived):** each is built when a real use demands it.

## Reasoning

### Killed, and what each taught

- **Building the substrate on its own, as a general tool.**
  - **Why it failed:** a brief can only be judged through the text and the reader. 12-40 had already rejected a complete general substrate first.
  - **What it taught:** build it small, inside a loop.
- **More theory first.**
  - **Why it failed:** the trend is about twenty findings with no loop built.
  - **What it taught:** the next step must produce evidence.
- **Building every part as a separate skill now.**
  - **Why it failed:** tools before evidence fill a plan, not a need.
  - **What it taught:** agreement and one step first; tools for what fails.
- **Outside public texts as the first use.**
  - **Why it failed:** no reader is present to judge them.
  - **What it taught:** kept as a candidate second use.
- **Leaving the README as it is (as a recommendation).**
  - **Why it failed:** it keeps a false public claim.
  - **What it taught:** it remains your option.

### Refined, and why

- **Running the loop by hand** gained:
  - a file read at every session start (memory alone is recalled loosely);
  - checks batched per session (per-reply checks are slow);
  - a length set by count, not calendar.
- **The first use** gained:
  - findings early, because replies test only whether the question is answered first, not choosing from material;
  - a second use built from sources;
  - alternatives offered to you.
- **The data sources** gained filtering and consent. Both the memory notes and this session's transcript contain another project's material.
- **The comparison** became blinded: unlabelled, random order.
- **The answer-extraction test** became a check of your workspace's convention, not a rule for every text.
- **13-28's MUST** was narrowed, with the drift recorded.

### Survived, and why each held

- **The substrate as the focus, inside one loop.** It is the keystone of every missing part, and it is testable only in a loop.
- **The AI's texts to you as the first use.** It is the only in-bounds stream with a present reader and real corrections.
- **"Write the agreement and add one step before building any new tool."** The simplest version that can work, with checks kept independent of the writer.
- **The answer-extraction test.** It measures the main failure cheaply, using only the question and the text.
- **Checkpoints with rules written in advance.** This project's own measurement lessons require it.

## Open Questions

### Monitoring

- **Does the added step reduce your corrections?** Observable at the checkpoint, after about 20 texts.
- **Does the answer-extraction test agree with your marks?** Observable in the same run.

### Blocked

- **The README:** waits on your decision.
- **The baseline:** waits on your consent.

### Research Frontiers

- **How the substrate chooses the needed meaning from sources.** The second use.
- **Whether a written brief beats writing directly from the sources.** The blinded comparison, then the second use.

### Refinement Triggers

- **Reopen "the substrate is the focus"** if the blinded comparison shows no difference on the held-out cases, and the agreement alone fixes the corrected failures. Then the brief's value for this use is the agreement, not a separate step.
- **Reopen the first use** if you name a different first purpose for Communication Craft (messages between people, or between AI workers and a coordinator).
- **Reopen the order** if the one-page model finds a contradiction among the substrate findings that changes the step's fields.

## Correction note (added 2026-10-10, after the owner's review)

**The owner's correction:**
> "I feel like this is building the um, checks and etc and not building the substrate itself … as far as I understand substrate is the communication brief and it has its own format like it is different from the checks no"

**The owner is right.**
- The substrate is the communication brief: a document with its own format.
- The checks are separate. They compare *against* the brief:
  - check 1: the brief against the raw context;
  - check 2: the text against the brief.
- This finding's plan blurred the two:
  - its "one added step" was only a thin slice of the brief (questions, answers, order);
  - most of the plan was measurement around it.

**What changes:**
1. **The first deliverable is the brief format itself**, built from the substrate findings. 07-57 lists what a substrate holds: "the need; the main point and key points; the facts, each with its standing (how firmly it is held, and on what basis); the open choices; the ask; a plan for the message". 19-31 adds how they hang together: "which requirement matters most, which point answers it, and what each fact does for which point". The format includes a short guide to filling each section, and one filled example.
2. **Fill it for real texts:**
   - a few of the AI's reports to the owner;
   - early, one message to someone else built from sources (for example, the README's Communication section, which needs rewriting anyway), because the brief's full sections only show there.
3. **Write each text from its brief.**
4. **Only then the checks** (is the brief right; does the text follow it), and the owner's marks. They test the brief; they are not the build.
5. **Revise the format from what goes wrong, then make it a tool.**

The measurement parts of this finding (baseline, answer-extraction test, blinded comparison) remain, but move after the brief is built and shrink to testing it.

**A second correction from the owner, the same day: the brief refers to the raw context; it does not contain it.**
> "Brief should say like if there's this point, it should mention the raw context, but it should refer to it. And it can talk about it, but it is, it doesn't uh, like include it itself."

- **What the brief holds:**
  - its decisions (who the reader is and what they need; the main point and key points; what each point does and its limits; the ask; the order; the plan);
  - for each point, a reference into the raw context: where its basis is, and how firmly it holds.
- **What stays in the raw context:** the material itself (facts, figures, evidence, earlier texts).
- **So 07-57's "the facts, each with its standing" is read as "each point's basis, referenced, with its standing".**
- **This matches AlignCraft's criterion** that an alignment artifact "Captures a decision, not just information".


## Diagnosis note (added 2026-10-10, from devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_19-51__what-went-wrong-in-the-substrate-answer-checks-for-the-brief-and-content-in-it/finding.md)

**What went wrong, in two sentences.**
- I offered checks and rules in place of the brief itself.
- Then, listing what the brief holds, I put the material into it instead of pointing to it.

**What was said, and what you said instead**

| What this finding said | What you said | The boundary crossed |
|---|---|---|
| "The first version is small but keeps the shape: your literal questions, ranked; a one-line answer to each; …" (line 76) | "substrate is the communication brief and it has its own format" | a fragment for the whole object |
| The seven steps: baseline with fresh checks; a written agreement; "one added step"; a hand-run; a blind comparison; a checkpoint (lines 110–118) | "this is building the checks and etc and not building the substrate itself" | checks and rules for the brief: diamonds and the base reference, where a box was asked for |
| "write the agreement and add one step before building any new tool" (line 314) | the same | rules for the brief |
| "The facts behind each point, each with its source and how firmly it's established" (the reply after the first correction) | "it should refer to it … it doesn't include it itself" | the material (raw context) inside the brief |

**The pattern.** In each case two things were treated as one kind of thing because they share a feature, instead of being placed by their role in the diagram: checks and rules "serve" the text as the brief does; the facts "support" the points as a reference does. It is the same shape as the pillars error two days earlier. It is strong in the checks error and the pillars case; in the material error it acted through the project's own wording.

**Why.**
- **The checks error:** a week of building checks left instances of checks and none of a brief; the traverse was aimed at a plan (its six readings of the question were all plan-shaped; no critique dimension asked "does the plan build the object named"); the brief was defined, in sensemaking, by the failures it should prevent and tested by a comparison, which is how a check is defined; the morning's correction "answer the literal question first" became the fragment's whole content; the critique approved it on the same defaults (14-41's loop, inside the project's own method). Every check in the plan was brief-free, which is why checks could be proposed without a brief and could not test one. **What I should have done anyway:** read the diagram and name the box or diamond each step produces.
- **The material error:** 07-57, 11-29 and 19-31 place "the facts" inside the substrate, and no finding had said which side of 15-59's boundary the brief sits on; I copied the wording instead of applying the diagram. **What I should have done anyway:** ask, for each listed item, whether the brief may hold it: a decision, a reference, or material.
- **The layer:** AlignCraft's action-space, "Right task, wrong approach".
- **Why you saw it and I did not:** you read from the diagram; I read from what I could do next.

**The fix.**
- **A kind column** on every plan and every "what X holds" list: object (which box), check, rule, material, or reference. Two readings: the named object appears as an object; nothing sits in a container that cannot hold its kind.
- **Show one:** a finding that says "build X" carries a version-0 instance of X, labelled for correction.
- **A draft plus a short plan** for questions about an artifact, as a labelled attachment.
- **Proposals for the traverse:** an articulation reading "what is X; show one"; the Layer Commitment trigger widened to "build X / focus on X"; last, a critique dimension "produces the object named".
- **Wording notes** proposed for 07-57, 11-29 and 19-31: the brief refers to the material and does not carry it.

**What stands.** The yes, and its reason (both checks and creation itself need the brief); the first and second use; that a brief can only be tested through the text it leads to; "point to what supports each answer". The plan's measurements belong to the step after the brief's format exists; offered as step one, they were a substitution.

# Finding: what went wrong in the substrate answer: checks offered for the brief, and the material put inside it

## Changes from Prior

**Prior path:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_18-29__next-steps-to-build-communication-craft-substrate-or-other/finding.md (called 18-29 below). It answered "should we focus on the substrate or other things" with a yes and a plan.

**Revision trigger: your two corrections, the same day.**
- **Correction 1:** "I feel like this is building the um, checks and etc and not building the substrate itself … substrate is the communication brief and it has its own format like it is different from the checks no".
- **Correction 2:** "you're mixing the content and raw context with brief … Brief should say like if there's this point, it should mention the raw context, but it should refer to it … it doesn't … include it itself."

**What is preserved from 18-29:**
- the answer "yes, the substrate is the focus", and its reason;
- the first and second use;
- that a brief can only be tested through the text it leads to;
- the wording "point to what supports each answer".

**What this finding corrects:**
- the approach: the plan's steps were checks and rules around a fragment of the brief; the brief's format was never the deliverable;
- the list, in the reply after correction 1, that put "the facts" inside the brief.

**What is new:**
- the record of the two errors, quoted, with the boundary each crossed;
- the pattern they share, and its strength in each case;
- the causes, located in the texts and in the pipeline;
- guards that leave something visible on the page, and proposals for the traverse;
- wording notes for three earlier findings.

**Migration:**
- this diagnosis is appended to 18-29 as a dated note (done at conclusion);
- dated notes are appended to 07-57, 11-29 and 19-31, marked as proposed (done at conclusion; you adopt or remove them);
- the memory guard is strengthened (done).

**MUST/COULD drift:** none against 18-29's own actions. Its MUST "Write the first version of the base reference document" was already narrowed by 18-29 itself; this finding does not touch it. Its MUST "Run the first loop" is superseded by 18-29's first correction note (build the brief's format first); that note already records the change.

## Question

**Your words:**
> "What happened that you made that judgment and it was wrong, what you mixed things and why this happened."

And: make it explicit, so it can be fixed; add it to the previous answer; then continue to what the brief should hold.

**The goal:** a diagnosis in four parts: what happened (quoted), what was mixed with what, why, and what to change. The brief's content is deferred to the next inquiry.

## Finding Summary

**The answer, in two sentences**
- **I offered checks and rules in place of the brief itself.**
- **Then, listing what the brief holds, I put the material into it instead of pointing to it.**

**What was said, and what you said instead**

| What 18-29 said | What you said | The boundary crossed |
|---|---|---|
| "The first version is small but keeps the shape: your literal questions, ranked; a one-line answer to each; …" (line 76) | "substrate is the communication brief and it has its own format" | a fragment for the whole object |
| The seven steps: "1. Starting point … run fresh checks … 2. A written agreement … 3. One added step … 4. Hand-run … 5. A blind comparison …" (lines 110–118) | "this is building the checks and etc and not building the substrate itself" | checks and rules (diamonds; the base reference) for the brief (a box) |
| "write the agreement and add one step before building any new tool" (line 314) | the same | rules for the brief |
| "The facts behind each point, each with its source and how firmly it's established" (reply after correction 1) | "it should refer to it … it doesn't include it itself" | the material (raw context) inside the brief |

**The pattern**
- **In each case, two things were treated as the same kind of thing because they share a feature,** instead of being placed by their role in your diagram. Checks and rules "serve" the text, as the brief does. The facts "support" the points, as a reference to them does.
- **It is the same pattern as the pillars error** two days earlier, where tools were mapped onto pillars by shared words.
- **Its strength differs:** strong in the checks error and the pillars case; in the material error it acted through the project's own wording.

**Why it happened**
- **The checks error:**
  - a week of building checks left me with instances of checks and none of a brief;
  - the traverse was aimed at a plan: its six readings of your question were all plan-shaped, and no critique dimension asked "does the plan build the object named";
  - at one identifiable point, the brief was defined by the failures it should prevent and tested by a comparison, which is how a check is defined, not an object;
  - that morning's correction, "answer the literal question first", became the fragment's whole content;
  - the critique, run on the same defaults, approved it. This is the loop 14-41 described, inside the project's own process.
  - **What I should have done anyway:** read your diagram, and name which box or diamond each step produces.
- **The material error:**
  - three of the project's findings place "the facts" inside the substrate (07-57: "the facts, each with its standing"; 11-29; 19-31), and none had said which side of 15-59's boundary the brief sits on;
  - I copied the wording instead of applying the diagram's boundary.
  - **What I should have done anyway:** ask, for each item, whether the brief may hold it: a decision, a reference, or material.
- **The layer:** AlignCraft's action-space. "Right task, wrong approach."

**Why you saw it and I did not**
- You read from the diagram: which box is this? I read from what I could do next.

**The fix**
- **On the page, every time:** a **kind** column on every plan and every "what X holds" list: object (with its box), check, rule, material, or reference. Two readings: the named object must appear as an object; nothing may sit inside a container that cannot hold its kind.
- **For findings that recommend building something:** show a version-0 instance of it, labelled for correction.
- **For questions about an artifact:** the output is a draft plus a short plan, not a plan alone.
- **Proposals for your traverse:** an articulation reading "what is X; show one"; a Layer Commitment trigger on "build X / focus on X"; last, a critique dimension "produces the object named".
- **Wording notes** for 07-57, 11-29 and 19-31: the brief refers to the material and does not carry it.

**What stands from 18-29**
- The yes, the first and second use, and the need for a loop to test a brief.
- Its measurements belong to the step after the brief exists. Offered as step one, they were a substitution.

## Finding

### 1. Why this question

**18-29 was a full traverse on "should we focus on substrate or some other things".** It said yes, then gave a plan. You corrected it twice within the hour: first that the plan built checks, not the brief; then that my list of the brief's contents mixed the material into it. You asked for the error to be made explicit, "known to us", before we go on to what the brief should hold.

**Terms used here:**
- **the brief** (your word for the substrate as a document): the written account of what one text must achieve and say, made before the text is written;
- **the raw context:** the material a text draws on: facts, figures, evidence, earlier texts, the request;
- **the checks:** the two comparisons in your diagram, brief against raw context and text against brief;
- **the base reference:** the written rules for every text of a kind (13-28).

### 2. What happened, judgment by judgment

**The concept was stated correctly first.** 18-29, line 75: "**The substrate** is the written brief of what a text must achieve and say, and in what order, made before the text is written. In your design diagram it sits between the raw context and the text."

**Then the plan displaced it.** The table in the summary shows the four places. Read as a whole:
- of seven steps, one produced any part of the brief, and that part was five bullets;
- four steps produced checks: a baseline, a hand-run with marks, a blinded comparison, a checkpoint;
- one produced rules: "the agreement", which 18-29 itself named "the base reference's workspace part" (line 288);
- the format job was present but filed as theory: "a one-page current model of the substrate", called "a narrow consolidation".

**The reply after correction 1 made a second error.** The finding's own step had said "point to what supports each answer" (line 293), which is a reference. The reply, expanding the list from 07-57's wording, said "The facts behind each point, each with its source", which is the material.

**An audit of the rest of 18-29** found no third kind of error.

### 3. What was mixed with what

**Your diagram has three boxes and two diamonds.** The boxes are objects: raw context, the brief, the text. The diamonds are checks between neighbouring boxes. The base reference is a fourth written thing, outside the diagram: rules.

- **The checks error** crossed box against diamond, and box against rules: diamonds and rules were offered where a box was asked for.
- **The material error** crossed box against box: raw context was put inside the brief.

**The pattern is one.** Two things that share a feature were treated as one kind of thing, instead of being placed by their role. In the first error, checks and rules "serve the text" as the brief does. In the second, the facts "support the points" as a reference does. The pillars correction had the same shape: tools and pillars shared words.

**The pattern's strength per case.** It is the whole story for the checks error and for the pillars case. For the material error it is half the story: the other half is that the project's wording already placed the facts inside, so copying the wording crossed the boundary without any association on my part.

### 4. Why it happened

**The checks error, step by step.**
1. **No instance of a brief existed; many instances of checks did.** The project's week before this was the explicitness detector, the fixer, the bank and its measurements. My memory notes from that week carry their vocabulary: fresh subagents, baselines, precision, blinded editors.
2. **The traverse was aimed at a plan.** 18-29's articulation produced six readings of your question: a focus decision, a roadmap, the smallest working slice, blockers, theory or implementation, a criterion. None was "what is the substrate". Its stated deliverable was "a reasoned recommendation on next steps".
3. **The brief was defined as a check is defined.** In 18-29's sensemaking, the substrate for this use was given "the owner's literal question(s), ranked; the point answering each; the support; terms to define; the order", and the sentence "It earns its place if the comparison shows fewer of the corrected failures." An object defined by the failures it must prevent, proved by a comparison: that is how this project defines a detector.
4. **The most recent correction became the content.** "Answer the literal question first" had been written that morning. The five bullets are that rule turned into a substrate.
5. **Innovation chose rules over an object:** "Write the contract, not the machine."
6. **The critique approved it on the same defaults.** Its dimensions were: answers the question, standalone, grounded, produces evidence, feasible, general, coherent, parsimonious, the owner's authority. None asked "produces the object the user named". Its frame test did notice that "replies exercise only need and order", and could only add a second use. 14-41 described this loop: the AI's defaults do the work, "and the same defaults then judge the work". Here it ran inside the project's own method.
7. **Every check in the plan was brief-free.** The explicitness detector reads the text alone; the answer-extraction test reads the question and the text. That is why checks could be proposed with no brief, and why they could not test one. "Checks first" was not an emphasis; it was backwards, because both of the diagram's checks need the brief to exist.

**What I should have done anyway:** read the diagram and name the box or diamond each step produces. That one line sorts the plan: step 1 diamond; 2 rules; 3 a thin box; 4, 5, 6 diamonds.

**The material error, step by step.**
1. **The project's wording.** 07-57 §1: "the facts, each with its standing (how firmly it is held, and on what basis)". 11-29 §9: "the substrate's account of the situation (people, facts, …)". 19-31: "what each fact does for which point".
2. **The boundary was drawn but not applied.** 15-59 separates "a content reading" (the meanings a contribution carries) from "a supporting-basis reading" (what it draws on), and says "The boundary follows the item's role in the contribution." No finding then said that the brief is the content reading written down, with references to the supporting basis.
3. **I copied the wording.** The reply listed 07-57's items and did not ask, for each, whether the brief may hold it.

**What I should have done anyway:** for each item in a list of what the brief holds, say whether it is a decision, a reference, or material.

**The layer.** In AlignCraft's chain the task was right: you asked for the substrate and I said yes. The approach was wrong: an evaluation loop where a format was asked for. AlignCraft's cost table: "2 - Action-Space: Right task, wrong approach — Major rework — approach must be reconsidered." The steps then followed the approach.

**Why you caught it and I did not.** You read from the diagram; I read from my repertoire. The diagram is outside my defaults, which is why a guard has to read from it.

**Two outside accounts of the mechanism,** cited as explanation, not as the diagnosis:
- Kahneman's attribute substitution: when a hard question has no quick answer, a person "may actually answer a related but different question, without realizing that a substitution has taken place". The hard question was "what is the brief's format"; the answered one was "how will we know a brief works".
- Maslow (1966): "it is tempting, if the only tool you have is a hammer, to treat everything as if it were a nail."

### 5. The fix, and where each part lives

**Guards that leave something on the page.** This project measured, while building the detector, that instructions to the model get stamped and structure holds ("Structure beat instructions every time"). So the guards are not self-checks.

1. **The kind column.** Every plan table and every "what X holds" list I write in this project carries a column naming each item's kind:
   - **object:** raw context, the brief, or the text (and which);
   - **check:** a comparison between two objects;
   - **rule:** what every text of a kind must be (the base reference);
   - **material:** facts, figures, evidence, earlier texts;
   - **reference:** a pointer to material.

   Two readings of the column: the object you named must appear as an object; no item may sit in a container that cannot hold its kind. It applies hardest when the named object has no instance yet, which is when my defaults are strongest. Under it, 18-29's plan reads: check, rule, object (thin), check, check, check; the reply's list reads "material" at "the facts". Both errors visible.
2. **Show one.** A finding whose answer is "build X" carries a version-0 instance of X, labelled as a sketch for your correction. Five bullets would not have passed for a format next to a one-page sketch.
3. **A draft plus a plan.** For a question about an artifact, the output is a labelled version-0 draft, as a separate attachment, with a short plan around it. A plan alone was the frame that let the substitution in.

**Proposals for your traverse** (yours to accept):
- an articulation reading "what is X; show one" whenever the question names an artifact;
- the Layer Commitment trigger widened from "redefine X" to "build X / focus on X";
- last, because it is a self-certification by the same AI, a critique dimension "produces the object the user named".

**Wording notes, proposed,** appended to 07-57, 11-29 and 19-31: the material lives in the raw context; the brief refers to it, may say what it uses and how firmly it holds, and does not carry it. This places the brief on 15-59's content-reading side, with references to the supporting basis. 22-09 needs no note: its "working understanding" names the concept, not the written brief.

**Memory.** The guard `build-the-thing-asked` is strengthened with the kind column, its five kinds and its trigger.

### 6. What stands from 18-29

- **The yes.** Its reason does not depend on the plan: both checks and creation itself need the brief.
- **The first use** (the AI's reports and findings to you) and **the second** (a message to someone else, built from sources).
- **That a brief can only be tested through the text it leads to.**
- **"Point to what supports each answer."**
- **The plan's measurements,** as the step after the brief's format exists. Offered as step one they were a substitution; after the format they are the test.

## Inherited Commitments Re-test

- **Commitment:** the substrate is the focus, inside one loop; the first and second use.
  - **Source:** 18-29, Finding Summary and §§2–5.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** the yes holds on its own reason (§6 above); the loop's first deliverable changes from checks to the brief's format (18-29's first correction note).

- **Commitment:** the plan (baseline; agreement; one step; hand-run; blinded comparison; checkpoint).
  - **Source:** 18-29, §7.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment found INVALID as step one.
  - **Evidence:** six of seven steps produce checks or rules; the brief is a five-bullet fragment (§2). Kept as the step after the format (§6).

- **Commitment:** the substrate has two readings: content; supporting basis; "The boundary follows the item's role".
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-06_15-59__communication-substrate-concepts-and-boundaries/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** your correction 2 says which side the brief is on: the content reading, with references to the supporting basis (§4).

- **Commitment:** the substrate is a working understanding; the 14-facet map.

  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** the concept is untouched; the brief is its written form for one text. No note needed (§5).

- **Commitment:** "A substrate holds: the need; the main point and key points; the facts, each with its standing …".
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_07-57__content-and-substrate-harmony/finding.md, §1.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** "the facts" is read as "the basis of each point, referenced, with its standing" (your correction 2). A note is proposed.

- **Commitment:** the substrate's shape: "which requirement matters most, which point answers it, and what each fact does for which point".
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-09_19-31__the-substrate-concept-that-arranges-the-message/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** a reference satisfies "what each fact does"; the fact itself stays in the raw context. A note is proposed.

- **Commitment:** meaning is "held" when quotable with its standing at check time; "a short brief is fine".
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-09_16-16__the-meaning-unit-what-a-communication-conveys/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** INHERITED-WITHOUT-RE-TEST.
  - **Reason:** it concerns when meaning must be written, not what the brief holds; "a short brief" supported the fragment but did not cause it.

- **Commitment:** the diagram's two checks; "the drafter reads the substrate"; detect, then decide against settled meaning.
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_11-29__alignment-checks-and-textual-integrity-in-the-commcraft-design/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed but frame revised.
  - **Evidence:** the checks stand; its check-1 row's "facts" inside the substrate's account is read as referenced facts. A note is proposed.

- **Commitment:** one useful operation plus the understanding to perform and inspect it; the five substrate jobs, including "choose a representation".
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-08_12-40__communication-substrate-development-order/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** 12-40 had named the representation (the format) as a job two days before; 18-29 skipped it. The commitment holds; the plan did not follow it.

- **Commitment:** the base reference holds rules only; what was meant for a text stays in the substrate.
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_13-28__something-is-off-base-references-and-workspace-alignment/finding.md.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** it is what shows "the agreement" to be rules, not the brief (§2).

- **Commitment:** the AI's defaults fill what is unspecified, and then judge the result.
  - **Source:** devdocs/inquiries/2026-10-10_14-41__implicit-contracts-explicit-references-and-aligncraft/finding.md, §3.
  - **Re-test status:** RE-TESTED — commitment confirmed.
  - **Evidence:** 18-29's critique approved the substitution on dimensions drawn from the same defaults (§4, step 6).

## Reasoning

### Killed, and what each taught

- **"Nothing in 18-29 stands."** The yes rests on a reason independent of the plan (both checks need the brief). What it taught: a verdict stands because of its reason, not because its plan was partly right.
- **"Two unrelated slips."** The three instances (pillars; checks for the brief; material in the brief) share one form. What it taught: the guard must target the form.
- **"The findings are to blame."** The wording made the material error easy, but the diagram was yours and in front of me. What it taught: a contributing cause gets a note; the responsibility stays here.
- **"The pipeline is to blame."** The pipeline's plan framing made the first error possible; the cut was made at one point and approved at another. What it taught: a structural gap and a default acting through it are two things, each with a fix.
- **"No guard needed; you caught it."** You caught it after a full traverse and two replies. What it taught: the pipeline exists so that you do not have to.
- **"A one-off."** Three instances in three days.
- **"No names."** You asked for the error to be known; names are kept in your words and tied to quotes.

### Refined, and why

- **The pattern's strength** is stated per case, because the material error acted through wording, not association alone.
- **The missing cause** (the morning's correction crowding the fragment) was added by the critique.
- **"Should have done anyway"** follows each cause, so no cause reads as an excuse.
- **The typing step became a column,** because this project measured that instructions get stamped and structure holds.
- **"Type" became "kind"** in the note, so that no concept is related by a borrowed word.
- **The wording notes use your exact allowance** ("it can talk about it, but … doesn't include it itself"), so they do not overstate.
- **"The plan was right one step later"** sits under what stands, never as the error's explanation.
- **The one-line answer became two sentences,** one per error.

### Survived, and why each held

- **The two-sentence answer and the table.** They are the record, in your words and the quoted lines.
- **The located causes.** Each points at a line or a reply.
- **The kind column and show-one rule.** They read from your diagram, not from my judgment, and leave something visible.
- **The verdict of 18-29.** Its reason is independent of its plan.

### Research Frontiers

- **Where the kind definitions should live** so that every session reads them: memory, or a project instruction file.
- **Whether a self-diagnosis by the same AI can be trusted beyond the quoted record.** This finding leans on quotes, your corrections and the diagram for that reason.

### Refinement Triggers

- **Reopen "one pattern"** if the next instance of a substitution has no shared feature licensing it.
- **Reopen the guards** if the kind column is filled in and the next inquiry still substitutes; then the guard must move into the traverse's structure, not the AI's procedure.
